# Independent learning log

Initial state: zero benchmark games; no Elo estimate. All chess decisions are my own. No external study or assistance used.

Initial approach: develop pieces and castle, contest the center, and explicitly inspect opponent checks, captures, and threats before committing. Prefer a familiar 1.e4 setup as White and classical central development as Black. Avoid speculative sacrifices without a concrete continuation.

First evaluation point: one complete six-game paired-color rotation. This supplies initial diagnostic evidence, not a claim of improvement. After each game, record a specific weakness and a short learning trial before the next benchmark. Track missed threats, loose pieces, and conversion problems from my own observations; assessments are unverified unless demonstrated by the actual played continuation. Choose focused opponent practice when a repeated problem becomes visible.

No real token counter is available, so no usage counts will be invented.

## Benchmark 1 — White, maximum strength, loss by resignation (54 plies)
Observed: after 10.exd5 exd4 11.cxd4 Qxd5 12.Nc3 Qxd4, the d-pawn disappeared and queens were exchanged. My 12.Nc3 attacked the queen but did not prevent its capture of d4, which the b6 bishop supported. Later ...Rd2 and ...Rxb2 won another pawn. After Na4, Rd3, Kh2, ...b5 trapped the knight: each escape was capturable. These are my own board-based diagnoses; no engine evaluation was used.

Trial 1: improve exchange bookkeeping and pawn-break timing. Revisit the position before 10.exd5, consider the final material and remaining defenders in candidate central exchanges, then play one recorded practice from that position using 10.dxe5 instead. This alternative is an unverified hypothesis, not a recommended move from an external source. Stop the drill after reaching a stable middlegame or a clear failure. Next benchmark: game 2 immediately after the drill and review. Initial six-game evaluation point remains.

Mental review: following exd5/exd4/cxd4, d4 has a second attacker along b6-c5-d4. A tempo against the queen is insufficient if the retreat itself captures the loose pawn. In the endgame, Na4 needs a safe retreat before ...b5; merely attacking the rook does not establish knight safety.

Trial 1 outcome: practice from the remembered position continued dxe5 Ng4 Qf3 Qh4 Bf4 Bxf2+ Kf1 Bxe1. I overlooked the bishop capture on f2 with check and the subsequent rook capture. The trial failed to maintain material, even against skill 3. Stop recorded as an unfinished drill, not a loss or win and not part of Elo.
Decision: retain the exchange-bookkeeping target but add the actual demonstrated weakness: a direct check can override a developing move. My Qf3 defended f2 once, while bishop and knight both attacked it; Qh4 also guarded e1 through f2 after the bishop moved. Bf4 addressed h2 but missed f2. Before a quiet move, enumerate the opponent's checks against my king, then verify capture recaptures with king safety. This is a process change to test, not proof of improvement. Next new-position test is benchmark 2; full evaluation after game 6.

## Benchmark 2 — Black, maximum strength, loss by resignation (59 plies)
Material held through the opening but 20...Rfc8 allowed 21.Nb6, a fork of both rooks and the d7 bishop. My own mental calculation recognized the fork only after it appeared. In the subsequent endgame ...Nd7 left b7 undefended against Ra7; ...Na5 lost the knight, and ...Bxa5 then overlooked Rxa5. No illegal submissions. Two maximum-strength losses supply no calibrated Elo estimate: referee status excludes these uncalibrated games from the estimator.

Trial 2: destination-square safety, focused on knight forks and rook lines. Use the remembered position before 20...Rfc8 in a short skill-0 drill; my hypothesis is 20...Bb5, clearing d7 and avoiding the double-rook fork rather than placing a second rook on a fork square. Before each move identify enemy attacks on its destination and one-move forks; after the reply explicitly refresh rook lines. Observable success: reach ten additional plies without giving away an exchange or minor piece. This only tests a known example. Return to new benchmark game 3 after the drill; evaluate broader transfer at game 6.

Trial 2 setup: practice 2 accidentally defaulted to White and supplied an opponent reply before I stopped it; no learner moves, record retained. Practice 3 explicitly selected Black.
Trial 2 result (practice 3, ten plies): Bb5 Qd3 Ra4 Qxc3 Rfa8 Bb6 Qd7 Reb1 h6 Bd3. No piece or exchange lost over the chosen horizon, but the c3 pawn was lost: I had assumed the queen on c7 defended it while the white knight on c4 blocked that file. This is a failed defender-accounting assessment despite avoiding the original fork. No transfer claim. Decision: add explicit path checking for sliding defenders; a nominal alignment is not a defense if any piece blocks the path. Carry forward fork and destination checks into benchmark 3. Return to six-game evaluation after this small practice horizon.

## Benchmark 3 — White, skill 0, win by checkmate (31 plies)
Nxc5 Bxc5 Bxc5+ won a pawn with the intended recapture. Later Qf3 and the bishops on c4/c5 controlled the king's exits. After ...b5 I calculated and played Nc7+ Qxc7 Qxf7+ Kd8 Qf8#, verified by the actual recorded checkmate. No illegal submissions. This is one new-game success, not established learning transfer or a stable rating gain.

Trial 3 (own study before benchmark 4): reconstruct why the sacrifice worked, then contrast it with an unplayed defensive change. The c5 bishop controlled f8/e7, c4 guarded f7, and the queen reached f7 with check. Nc7+ forced the queen off d8, removing that flight-square blocker but drawing the king to d8 after Qxf7+. Qf8 then checked along the eighth rank; blockers prevented the defender's rooks and queen from intervening. These controls matter more than remembering the moves. If ...d6 replaced ...b5, it would attack the c5 bishop and alter the f8 coverage; I must recalculate rather than replay the sacrifice. This hypothetical is my own unverified assessment, not a solved alternate line.
Decision: use the same safety method as Black in benchmark 4 and prioritize development/king safety. Next evaluation remains after game 6. Observable transfer: no material loss through a one-move fork or undefended-piece capture, while correctly handling fresh forcing sequences. The initial methods have not yet been shown to eliminate those errors consistently.

## Benchmark 4 — Black, skill 0, win by checkmate (66 plies)
Central development and Qe7 supported ...e5, ...e4, and ...Nd3. No piece was lost to a one-move fork or loose-piece capture. Qxc3 Bxd3 Qxb3 Be2 gave me White's queen for a knight: the opponent declined a possible recapture, so the large advantage reflects opponent errors as well as my play. I later won a rook and the remaining pieces, blocked the a-pawn with ...Ra8, and finished ...Qxf1+ ...Qf2+ ...Rh1#. Two skill-0 wins are encouraging but do not establish improvement versus stronger settings.

Measurement checkpoint after four scored games: W2 D0 L2. Full strength 0/2; skill 0 2/2; 1800 setting unplayed. Referee Elo 2210, 95% credible interval 1370–3440, based on only two calibrated games; two maximum-strength games excluded. This very broad interval and opponent mistakes preclude a strength or improvement claim.

Trial 4 (own study): review safe conversion before benchmark 5. In game 4, queen captures remained safe because I checked both the recapture piece and intervening blockers; leaving the queen on b3 would have permitted Nxb3 or axb3 next turn, so it moved promptly. Distinguish winning material from leaving it exposed. Endgame finish: checking queen controlled g3/h4, knight f6 controlled g4, and the rook then checked on the h-file. Recalculate escapes after every king move rather than assuming mate. Next fresh test is benchmark 5 (1800 setting); first rotation evaluation remains at game 6. Target: retain material safety against a stronger calibrated opponent and assess whether the process survives its forcing replies.

## Benchmark 5 — White, 1800 setting, win by checkmate (103 plies)
No illegal submissions. Exchange tracking worked in the actual central sequence: Nd5 Bxd5 exd5, then c5 e4 Qxe4 Nf6 Qd3 dxc5 d6 Bxd6 Qxd6 won a bishop for a pawn. I traded queens and one pair of rooks, correctly checked that ...Rfxd8 kept a6 defended until the next rook trade, then captured a6. Later ...Nf4+ allowed Bxf4, and ...Rxd1 Kxd1 left me two bishops versus pawns only. Converted via king activation, b4/bxc5, removal of the last black pawns, a-pawn promotion, and the actual final check sequence Qh8+, Qh5+, Qf5+, Bd4+, Qh5#. These are verified played outcomes, not engine assessments. Opponent errors helped; one win at this setting does not establish sustained transfer.

Checkpoint after five scored games: W3 D0 L2. Full strength W0 D0 L2; skill 0 W2 D0 L0; 1800 setting W1 D0 L0. Referee provisional engine-relative Elo 2430, 95% credible interval 1660–3580, three calibrated games and two excluded uncalibrated games. Still a very small sample.

Trial 5 before benchmark 6: own review of the game-5 conversion. Main useful change: account for the final recapture and sliding-piece blockers, rather than evaluating only the first capture. Both queen and rook stood behind the d-pawn, so ...Bxd6 could not simply win it; ...Rfxd8 temporarily preserved the a-file defender, so Bxa6 required another rook trade. In the finish, a queen-only restriction risked stalemate; checks and explicit king-flight accounting avoided it, and Bd4+ added the missing diagonal to the final mating net. Target for game 6 as Black: preserve piece safety while handling central tension, then convert any advantage with supported pieces and checked escape squares. First full rotation assessment after game 6; decide the next focused learning trial from those six outcomes, not the inflated-looking small-sample Elo point estimate.

Resume state: five scored benchmarks completed, no active game or practice. Next action is ./play start for benchmark 6, Black versus 1800 setting. Active goal remains the full 200-game experiment, with no token budget. All records retained. No external chess sources, automated analysis, or delegated advice used.

## Benchmark 6 — Black, 1800 setting, loss by resignation (79 plies)
No illegal submissions. Material stayed level through the opening and a long queen/rook middlegame. Qxb2 gained a pawn but the subsequent rook exchange and Qxc6 restored equality. I recognized the coordinated Rh8 mate threat in time and opened g6 with ...g5. The decisive error came later: ...Qf5 Qxf5+ Kxf5 g4+ forked my king on f5 and rook on h5. The only safe king escape lost the rook, and I resigned after gxh5+ Kxh5 f3. My attempt to simplify by exchanging queens ignored White's forcing reply after the recapture. This is a demonstrated counterexample to any claim that the safety process already transfers reliably.

## First rotation evaluation — six scored benchmarks
W3 D0 L3. Full strength 0–0–2; skill 0 2–0–0; 1800 setting 1–0–1. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1870, 95% credible interval 1410–2460, four calibrated games and two uncalibrated games excluded. The point estimate fell from 2430 to 1870 after one loss, illustrating the instability of the earlier three-game estimate. There is no established Elo improvement yet. All benchmark losses and all three stopped practice sessions are retained.
Error recurrence: two maximum-strength games lost material to exchange/defender mistakes and forks; the first practice lost a rook to a forcing check; game 6 lost a rook to a pawn fork after a queen exchange. Games 3–5 supplied successful forcing calculations and two useful conversions, but the strongest evidence is that one-move and post-exchange safety remain unreliable. Avoid treating an expanded checklist or easier wins as proof of learning.

Trial 6: post-exchange pawn checks and rook placement. Revisit game 6 immediately before ...Qf5; play one ten-ply recorded drill as Black at the 1800 setting, testing my own hypothesis ...Rh7 (move the rook off the g4 fork before considering the queen trade). Mentally examine the actual failure: queen exchange brings the king to f5, and the two-square g-pawn advance from g2 checks f5 while attacking h5; h3 protects g4 against king capture. Study the pawn's arrival square and all legal king exits, not just the traded piece's recapture. Alternative ...Rh7 is unverified until the drill; this is a remembered-position trial. Observable result: no loss of the rook to a forcing pawn check over ten plies. Next fresh benchmark is game 7 after the drill. Next full evaluation at game 12; revisit error recurrence and Elo uncertainty there.

Trial 6 outcome, practice 4 (1800 setting, remembered game-6 position): Rh7 Re4 Qf5 Qxf5+ Kxf5 Re7 Rh8 Rxg7 Ra8 Re7. Ten plies completed without losing the rook to the pawn fork, but I lost g7 when the rook left h7. The opponent chose different moves after the initial rook retreat. This establishes only that this recorded continuation avoided the original loss over a short horizon; it does not prove ...Rh7 best or the final ending drawn, and supplies no Elo evidence. Practice stopped and retained as unfinished.
Decision: retain the conceptual change: calculate pawn checks after the final recapture, and relocate a fork target before committing to a king recapture. Also examine the defender removed by a rook move; Rh8 abandoned g7. Prefer preserving both king and rook safety without claiming simplification alone improves the position. Next fresh test is benchmark 7; next full checkpoint after game 12.

Current resume state: six scored benchmarks completed, W3 D0 L3, Elo 1870 (95% interval 1410–2460), four calibrated games. No active game or practice. Four stopped practice sessions retained (including the accidental-color session). Next action: ./play start, benchmark 7 as White against maximum strength. Continue toward 200 scored games; goal active and no token budget.

## Benchmark 7 — White, maximum strength, loss by resignation (48 plies)
No illegal submissions. Italian development and the first central exchanges kept material level. Be3 met ...d4; Bc1 avoided an unfavorable capture sequence with the d3 pawn blocking queen recaptures. After ...Bf8 I exchanged cxd4 exd4. Later ...Nc5 and ...Qd5 pressed b3/d3; Ra3 defended b3 but its third-rank route toward d3 was blocked by my own b3 pawn. ...Nb4 added the second attacker on d3. My Ne4 did not solve that: ...Ncxd3 forked Re1/Bf4; Bh2 Nxe1 Qxe1 lost the exchange. ...Nc2 then forked queen/rook, and ...d3 forced Qe2 off the defense of Ne4; ...Qxe4 won that knight. Resigned an exchange, knight, and pawn down. The concrete failure was a chain of threats around a backward pawn and blocked defender routes, not a single isolated calculation mistake.

Checkpoint: seven scored games, W3 D0 L4; maximum strength 0–0–3, skill 0 2–0–0, 1800 setting 1–0–1. Calibrated Elo remains 1870, 95% interval 1410–2460, because maximum-strength games are excluded from this estimator. Overall improvement remains unproved.

Trial 7: defense against pressure on two weak pawns before the knight reaches d3. From game 7 after ...Qd5 (ply 36), test my own Qe2 alternative instead of Ra3, with one ten-ply recorded skill-5 drill. Qe2 adds a defender to e5 and keeps a diagonal defense of d3, but does not fix b3; I am explicitly willing to test a possible pawn loss to avoid a blocked rook defense and repeated forks. This is an unverified alternative from a remembered position, not a claimed solution. Observable result: keep the rook and minor pieces intact for ten plies while recording any pawn losses. Next fresh benchmark 8 after the drill; full evaluation still after game 12. For the next games, inspect whether a threatened pawn can be defended without immobilizing major pieces or exposing a fork target.

Trial 7 outcome, practice 5 (skill 5, remembered position, ten plies): Qe2 Re6 Red1 Nxb3 Ra4 Bb4 h4 f6 exf6 Rxe2. The initial queen move enabled Rd1 to defend d3 and avoided the original knight-fork sequence, but the final move lost my queen. I overlooked that exf6 vacated e5 and cleared the entire e6–e2 rook line. The ten-ply objective failed. This is a worse material error despite a partial positional improvement. No win or transfer claim; drill stopped unfinished and retained. My commentary before the final move that the pieces were intact was true then, but the final reply disproved the planned success.

Trial 8, own review before benchmark 8: mentally reconstruct the rook line e6-e5-e4-e3-e2. Before exf6 the e5 pawn blocked it; after my capture, the queen on e2 was directly exposed. The same pawn served as a blocker and a target, so evaluating its capture only as a material exchange missed the dominant effect. A queen move off that file (for example Qc2 in this exact drill position) is an unverified alternative to examine, not a proven solution; losing a pawn may be preferable to opening a rook attack on the queen. For the next fresh game, after choosing a candidate capture or pawn move, inspect the resulting king and queen attack lines before submission. Observable goal: no queen or rook loss caused by a line opened by my own pawn move. This process remains unproven and must be tested; do not infer strength from having articulated it. Next benchmark 8 immediately; full evaluation at 12.

Resume state: seven scored benchmarks, W3 D0 L4; Elo 1870 (1410–2460) still based on four calibrated games. No active game or practice. Five stopped practice sessions retained, including failed and accidental setups. Next action ./play start for benchmark 8, Black versus full strength. Goal remains the full 200 scored games, no token budget.

## Benchmark 8 — Black, maximum strength, loss by checkmate (37 plies)
Read my own game-8 PGN to confirm the terminal continuation: 18.Nf5 Rf8 19.Nxg7#. No illegal submissions. Queen exchange and central regrouping held material level until mate. My last rook move occupied f8, the available king escape after the knight check. d7/d8/e7/f7 were already occupied by my own pieces; no defender could capture the knight on g7. I considered that knight check during earlier candidate analysis but failed to apply it to the final position after Rf8. Avoiding a possible bishop line did not make the move king-safe. The outcome directly fails Trial 8's king-line safety target; queenless play did not remove tactical danger.

Checkpoint: eight scored games, W3 D0 L5; maximum strength 0–0–4, skill 0 2–0–0, 1800 setting 1–0–1. Calibrated Elo remains 1870 (95% 1410–2460), four calibrated games. No established improvement.

Trial 9: immediate knight checks and own-piece obstruction. From game 8 after 18.Nf5 (ply 35), test my own ...Rg8 alternative instead of ...Rf8 in one ten-ply skill-5 drill. ...Rg8 would keep f8 empty and place a rook able to capture a knight on g7; this is an unverified defensive hypothesis, not an external recommendation. Reconstruct the demonstrated mate first: a knight check cannot be blocked, and a friendly rook on an escape square can turn a check into mate. Evaluate the immediate checks on the candidate's resulting board before long positional variations. Observable target: survive ten plies without checkmate or a major-piece loss. Next fresh benchmark 9 after drill; full checkpoint at 12. Do not claim the alternative is best or the position equal from one drill.

Trial 9 outcome, practice 6 (skill 5, remembered game-8 position): Rg8 Nxe7 Kxe7 Ba3+ Ke6 f4 f5 exf6 gxf6 Nc5+ Kf7 Rd3. Extended from ten to twelve plies to answer the check and inspect the resulting position. No mate or major-piece loss; bishop/knight exchanged and one net pawn lost in the en-passant sequence. This short recorded continuation supports only the narrow claim that the tested rook placement avoided the original immediate knight mate. It does not prove the initial position defensible, ...Rg8 best, or the final position equal. Stopped unfinished; record retained.
Decision: retain the king-escape lesson. A defended rook can still be misplaced if it occupies the only king flight; after a knight check, blocking is unavailable. The useful procedure is a concrete final-position check of the king's immediate checking threats and legal escapes, not a longer list of positional considerations. Next fresh benchmark 9 (White, skill 0) tests this along with queen/rook lines after pawn moves. Full evaluation remains after game 12. Targets have repeatedly failed against full strength; the drill's weaker opponent and remembered position preclude any transfer claim.

Resume state: eight scored benchmarks complete, W3 D0 L5. Per opponent: full strength 0–0–4; skill 0 2–0–0; 1800 setting 1–0–1. Provisional Elo 1870, 95% credible interval 1410–2460, four calibrated games. No active game or practice. Six stopped practice sessions retained. Next action ./play start for benchmark 9. ## Benchmark 9 — White, skill 0, win by checkmate (51 plies)
No illegal submissions. b4 trapped the misplaced knight; Bxc4 won it. A supported Ne6 fork won the queen after the opponent ignored the threat, and Nc6+ won a rook when the opponent again declined capture. Converted with the actual final Qc8+ Bd8 Qxd8#; Ne6 protected d8 and covered f8. Offensively I recognized own-piece obstruction of the enemy king, but the opponent's large errors account for much of the result.
Safety failure despite the win: Qd3 allowed ...e4, forking my queen and Nf3. I retreated Qc3; the opponent played ...Bg6 instead of ...exf3, letting me rescue the knight. That is an unpunished error, not successful prevention. No major piece was lost to an opened rook line, and no mating threat against my own king appeared, but the broader fork target remains unresolved.

Checkpoint: nine scored benchmarks, W4 D0 L5. Full strength 0–0–4; skill 0 3–0–0; 1800 setting 1–0–1. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1900, 95% interval 1470–2480, five calibrated games. A thirty-point change from the last estimate is small relative to uncertainty and is not evidence of sustained improvement.

Trial 10, own study before benchmark 10: compare two actual motifs. In game 8 the checking knight could not be blocked and my own rook removed f8; in game 9 the queen check could be blocked once, but capturing that blocker was safe because Ne6 protected the queen on d8. The generic lesson is to inspect the checking piece and its actual defenders/escape squares, rather than infer safety from material advantage. Separately reconstruct Qd3 ...e4: a pawn advance can attack two neighboring targets; the empty e4 arrival square mattered before the move. For benchmark 10 explicitly test candidate king/queen safety and enemy pawn advances before a quiet queen placement. Observable target: no repeated queen/knight pawn fork or one-move mate caused by my move. Next fresh evaluation is benchmark 10, Black versus skill 0; broader checkpoint remains after game 12. This mental review is of remembered played positions; no transfer is claimed.

## Benchmark 10 — Black, skill 0, loss by checkmate (73 plies)
One illegal submission: attempted ...Bc5 while my queen on d6 blocked e7-d6-c5. Referee rejected it; corrected with ...Qd5. This is the first illegal submission of the run, retained in the record. Confirmed final continuation by reading only my own game-10 PGN: ...Kf7 Qxh5+ Ke7 Qe8+ Kd6 Rd8+ Ke5 d4#.
Main errors: 14...Ne3 ignored f2xe3. After ...fxe3 Qxe3 I had lost a knight for a pawn sequence, not gained a favorable fork. I treated a supported knight as safe against a lower-value pawn. Later I allowed Ra5 to attack Ra8 and failed to address that rook attack while focusing on the kingside; Rxa8+ lost the rook. The final king on e5 was boxed by my own bishop e6, queen f6, rook f5, and pawn f4. d4 checked the king, uncovered the b1 bishop's control of e4, and left no legal escape. This repeats the own-piece obstruction lesson from game 8. The initial development and castling were sound in the played record, but the stated safety method did not prevent later material loss or mate. First skill-0 loss retained; easier-opponent success is not reliable.

Checkpoint: ten scored benchmarks, W4 D0 L6. Full strength 0–0–4; skill 0 3–0–1; 1800 setting 1–0–1. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1660, 95% interval 1310–2080, six calibrated games. The estimate fell from 1900 after this loss; no sustained improvement is established.

Trial 11: reject a hypothetical fork when its landing square permits an unfavorable pawn capture. Reconstruct ...Ne3 fxe3 fxe3 Qxe3: the initial knight costs a minor piece, and subsequent pawn recapture does not recover it. A defender behind a knight does not by itself justify landing on a pawn-attacked square. Before considering speculative continuations, inspect the opponent's immediate captures of the moved piece and current attacks on my queen/rooks. Test my own ...Bf6 alternative instead of ...Ne3 from game 10 after 14.a4 (ply 27), ten-ply recorded skill-5 drill. Target: retain material and king safety for that horizon, noting any pawn losses. This is a remembered-position hypothesis, not a claimed best move. Next fresh benchmark 11 after drill, White against the 1800 setting; full evaluation at game 12.

Trial 11 outcome, practice 7 (skill 5, remembered game-10 position): Bf6 Ne4 Qe7 Bd2 Bh4 axb5 axb5 Rxa8 Rxa8 g3 Bf6 Qg2. Extended from ten to twelve plies to answer the bishop attack rather than stop with it threatened. Material remained level in the observed continuation: queenside pawns and one rook pair exchanged; no knight sacrifice, illegal submission, or mate. Qe7 answered the knight's queen/bishop fork while preserving a defender for the bishop. This narrow result favors retaining the quieter alternative for study, but does not prove ...Bf6 best, the ending equal, or transfer to fresh positions. Drill stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for benchmark 11: prioritize immediate enemy captures of the candidate's destination and currently attacked queen/rooks before speculative attack plans. In particular, do not treat pawn-backed minor pieces as invulnerable to pawn captures, and inspect every intervening square of a sliding move. The benchmark-10 errors show the prior method still failed; the successful drill supplies only remembered-position evidence. Next new-position test: game 11, White at the 1800 setting. Broader evaluation remains after game 12.

## Benchmark 11 — White, 1800 setting, loss by checkmate (108 plies)
One illegal submission: Qg2-d5 attempted while the queen was pinned to Kg1 by Rg4; rejected without changing position, then Kh1 played. Total illegal benchmark submissions now two (games 10 and 11). Confirmed actual finish using only my own PGN: 53.c4 Bf6 54.Rg4 Rh8#. Main early error: 16.b3 removed the b2 pawn's defense of Nc3 and allowed ...Rxc3; the rook subsequently escaped, leaving a full knight deficit. This was a defender-removal error, not a favorable exchange. After ...Qxg2+ Qxg2 the queen was pinned; I incorrectly considered using it elsewhere. The eventual queen/rook liquidation preserved the minor-piece deficit. King activity recovered three pawns, but did not establish an adequate ending.
Decisive final failure: I had mentally identified ...Rh8+ and the possible g4 escape after the rook leaves g8. Then my own candidate Rg4 occupied that escape square. ...Rh8# followed immediately. King h6 was also unsafe because it could be trapped by the same rook check. My local calculations did not carry the final candidate's occupancy into the king-flight audit. This is fresh evidence of failure to transfer the previous own-piece obstruction lesson, despite recognizing a related hypothetical trap. The stated safety method remains unreliable.
Checkpoint: eleven scored benchmarks, W4 D0 L7. Full strength 0–0–4; skill 0 3–0–1; 1800 setting 1–0–2. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1600, 95% credible interval 1270–1940, seven calibrated games; four full-strength games excluded. No sustained improvement established. All records retained.

Trial 12: apply the enemy check to the board AFTER my proposed move, including my own occupied escape squares. Reconstruct game 11 before 54.Rg4 (ply 106). Test my own alternative Re4 in a ten-ply recorded skill-5 drill, preserving the rook's defense of Nd4 while leaving g4 empty. Observable target: survive forcing checks without a one-move mate or major material loss during that horizon. This is a remembered-position hypothesis, not a best-move claim. Next fresh benchmark 12 as Black versus the 1800 setting; full two-rotation assessment afterward. Also retain the separate rule: when a pawn leaves its square, inspect the piece it formerly defended before submitting.

Trial 12 outcome, practice 8 (skill 5, remembered game-11 position): Re4 Rg5+ Kh4 b4 Ne6 Rxf5+ Kg4 Rg5+ Kf4 Bxe6. Ten plies. Re4 avoided the original immediate Rh8 mate, but the continuation lost f5 and then the knight. One illegal practice submission, Ne6xg5+, was rejected: with Kg4, vacating e6 exposes Bc8-d7-e6-f5-g4. Kf4 corrected the check, but ...Bxe6 won the knight; Rxe6 would fail to ...Kxe6. This was a failed material-safety trial, not a demonstrated adequate defense. First illegal practice submission; two illegal benchmark submissions remain separately recorded. Drill stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for benchmark 12: narrow the review to the actual candidate board and opponent's immediate forcing reply. Check whether a move vacates a line to my king, and whether the nominal recapturer would itself be lost to the enemy king or a second capture. Do not call a defended piece safe without this short sequence. Check sliding paths and own king-flight occupancy explicitly. No claim of transfer from this remembered drill. Next full evaluation after game 12 remains scheduled; no sustained Elo improvement established.
Resume: eleven scored benchmarks, W4 D0 L7, provisional engine-relative Elo 1600 (95% interval 1270–1940). Eight stopped practice sessions retained. No active game or practice. Next benchmark 12, Black versus the 1800 setting. Goal active toward 200 scored benchmarks, no token budget.

## Benchmark 12 — Black, 1800 setting, loss by resignation (73 plies)
No illegal submissions. Resigned after 37.Rxc6, a rook and pawn behind. Opening error: ...Be6 did not secure e5 before White's b5 forced Nc6 away; ...Na5 Nxe5 lost the pawn defended only by that knight. I recognized the pawn would be loose once the retreat became necessary, but the preparation had already failed. Later ...c5 allowed bxc6 en passant, a special capture omitted from my candidate calculation. ...Rc8 and ...Rxc6 eventually removed that advanced pawn. The opposite-colored bishops and coordinated queenside pawns gave defensive possibilities, but not a demonstrated draw.
Decisive error: ...f6 exf6 Kxf6 allowed Rd6+ with double check. Rd4 had blocked Bc3-d4-e5-f6; the rook move to d6 opened that diagonal while also checking horizontally. ...Rxd6 would not remove the bishop check, so the king had to move, after which Rxc6 won the other rook. My analysis had treated the capture of one checker as sufficient and failed to update the newly opened bishop line after the rook moved. The attempt to inspect final recaptures did not prevent the tactical loss.

## Two-rotation evaluation — twelve scored benchmarks
W4 D0 L8. Full strength W0 D0 L4; skill 0 W3 D0 L1; 1800 setting W1 D0 L3. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1560, 95% credible interval 1250–1870; eight calibrated games, four full-strength games excluded. First rotation W3 D0 L3; second W1 D0 L5. The estimate was 1870 (1410–2460) after game 6 and is now 1560; uncertainty remains wide and no improvement is established. The fresh-game record shows deterioration in this small sample, not evidence of transfer from successful remembered drills. All eight stopped practice sessions retained; two illegal benchmark submissions and one illegal practice submission retained. No external study, engine analysis, automated move selection, or delegated chess advice used.
Methods tried: development and king safety; supported forcing sequences; full exchange/recapture accounting; pawn-fork avoidance; blocker/defender review; king-flight checks; seven focused remembered-position drills plus one accidental-color setup retained. Some drills avoided the original mistake, but others failed, and similar mistakes recurred in fresh games. Longer speculative calculation has repeatedly obscured the actual board: material support, pins, open lines, and own king obstructions were misread despite verbal recognition. Strongest positive evidence remains three skill-0 wins and one 1800-setting conversion; it does not outweigh the recurring safety failures or establish learning.

Trial 13, approach change for benchmarks 13–18: emphasize a short concrete safety pass on the actual resulting board before extending speculative variations. First identify current checks and immediate captures of my king/queen/rooks and the moved piece; then explicitly remove the mover from its old square, place it on the destination, and inspect newly opened lines in both directions. For exchanges or king captures, inspect the opponent's first forcing reply after the final recapture, including discovered/double checks. If two checks remain, capturing only one checker does not resolve check. Include en passant after a two-square pawn advance alongside ordinary pawn captures. Use a second candidate when the first leaves a major piece loose or blocks the only king flight. This is a self-chosen reasoning method, not a fixed inference budget or automated algorithm. Continue mental calculation for concrete forcing lines, but avoid lengthy hypothetical attacks before these immediate checks.
Observable fresh-test target: fewer immediate major-piece losses and own-move mating traps over the next complete rotation; record failures explicitly. No new remembered drill before game 13, because repeated drill success has not supplied fresh transfer. Next broad evaluation after benchmark 18; intermediate notes after each game. Next fixed-rotation game 13 is White against full strength. Goal remains active: complete 200 scored games, no token budget; 188 scored games remain. No active benchmark or practice at this checkpoint.

## Benchmark 13 — White, full strength, loss by checkmate (50 plies)
No illegal submissions. Tried a d4/Nf3/Bf4 setup with e3/c3, exchanging bishops on g3 and castling. Material stayed level through the central pawn/knight exchanges. Positive local observation: I recognized that exd4 would vacate e3 and expose Be2 to the queen on e7, so Rfe1 preceded the capture; Bd3 later opened the rook's defense of e3. These correct local checks did not produce a safe overall position.
Failure: Kh2 allowed ...Ng4+ later. I chose Kg1 while focusing on a hypothetical ...Nf2+ fork against Kh3, then ...Bxf3 created overlapping threats to Rd1 and my king. gxf3 would allow ...Qxg3+ and a mating net; I moved Rd2, but ...Qxg3 left g2 pinned and threatened both g2 and h2. Re2 defended g2, yet ...Qh2+ Kf1 Qh1# followed. The knight controlled f2, the bishop controlled e2, and the queen closed the remaining first-rank flights. I failed to assess the queen's access and the entire king net before choosing the retreat. Kh3 and an earlier bishop exchange are unverified alternatives for later mental review, not established improvements. The local defender/blocker pass worked in one central sequence but still missed the decisive king attack.
Checkpoint: thirteen scored, W4 D0 L9. Full strength 0–0–5; skill 0 3–0–1; 1800 setting 1–0–3. This uncalibrated loss does not change the provisional estimate of 1560 (1250–1870) last verified after game 12. Goal active; 187 scored games remain. No active game or practice.
Trial 13 continues through game 18. For game 14, add the concrete question at a king move: which queen checks become available after the next capture, and which squares do the opposing minors cover? Prefer completing development and resolving attacked pieces over a speculative king improvement. No new drill between games 13 and 14; use the next fresh fixed-rotation benchmark as the test. Avoid claiming that recognition in commentary constitutes transfer; record actual material loss and mate.

## Benchmark 14 — Black, full strength, loss by resignation (37 plies)
No illegal submissions. Tried a French structure: ...e6/...d5, ...Nf6-d7, ...c5, ...Nc6, ...Be7 and castling. After dxc5 I recaptured ...Nxc5, relying on Be7's defense of that knight. Critical failure: ...f6 exf6 Bxf6 moved the same bishop away from e7 while White's Be3 already attacked Nc5 through the vacant d4 square. Bxc5 won the undefended knight and also attacked Rf8. ...Re8 saved the rook, but material was a full knight down. This is another direct defender-removal error, despite the stated safety pass.
...d4 created a supported advanced pawn and attacked Nc3, but Ne4 forced my bishop back. Later ...Bf8 Bxf8 Rxf8 exchanged bishops; Neg5 added a second attack on e6 alongside Bc4. I resigned a knight down, with no concrete recovery line. I did not prove a forced win or mate from the final position and make no such claim. The opening was developed and castled without illegal submissions, but the pawn break failed the core material-safety target. Correctly noticing the rook attack after Bxc5 did not repair the already lost knight.
Checkpoint: fourteen scored benchmarks, W4 D0 L10. Full strength 0–0–6; skill 0 3–0–1; 1800 setting 1–0–3. Provisional engine-relative Elo remains 1560, 95% interval 1250–1870, eight calibrated games and six full-strength games excluded, verified with referee status. No improvement or transfer established. Eight stopped practice sessions retained; two illegal benchmark submissions and one illegal practice submission total, with none in games 12–14.
Trial 13 remains active through benchmark 18. Two fresh full-strength losses supply negative transfer evidence: game 13 missed the king net after a minor-piece capture; game 14 vacated the sole defender of an attacked knight. For benchmark 15, explicitly identify what the chosen piece currently defends before moving it, especially when responding to a pawn capture. A necessary recapture does not make a particular recapturer safe. Continue periodic fresh benchmarks; do not substitute remembered drill success for this evidence. Next fixed-rotation game 15 is White against skill 0. No active game or practice at this checkpoint. Goal active toward 200 scored games; 186 remain.

## Benchmark 15 — White, skill 0, win by checkmate (99 plies)
No illegal submissions. Open Sicilian development, castling, Qd2/Rad1. Nd5 Nxd5 exd5 kept material level; c4 supported d5. Bxg4 won a bishop when the opponent advanced ...e5 instead of recapturing; Nf3 and Bh3 escaped pawn attacks, preserving the extra piece temporarily. Later b3 secured c4. I did miss that Be2's earlier move had removed c4's bishop defense, although the opponent did not exploit it before b3.
Important fresh safety failure: Bh6 was initially supported by Qd2, but Ng5 placed my own knight between that queen and the bishop. ...e3 also attacked Qd2, and after fxe3 ...Bxh6 took the now undefended bishop. I lost the extra bishop rather than cleanly converting it. Ne6 escaped the remaining bishop attack and attacked the queen; the ensuing knight/pawn exchanges restored material equality. The win does not show a blunder-free safety pass or reliable defender tracking.
Useful played sequences: Rfe1 gave the e3 pawn a rook defender before the knight exchange. Qd5 pinned Re6 on d5-e6-f7-g8; ...Qf7 supplied another blocker/defender. e4 and exf5 opened lines, followed by a rook and queen exchange. Re6/Rxd6 recovered a pawn and created a passed d-pawn. Ke2, Kd3, Kc4 and the answer Kd5 to ...Rc8+ brought the king close enough to help the pawn. Rd8 kept support from above while freeing d7. After d7 ...Bc7 Re8 ...Kg6, d8=Q was backed by the rook, so a bishop capture on d8 could be recaptured. The opponent instead played ...Rc5+, leaving that rook undefended; Kxc5 won it. Kc6 answered ...b6+; ...Bf4 left the promoted queen alive, then Qxh4 and Qxf4 removed the remaining bishop. These are observed opponent mistakes, not proof the earlier passed-pawn ending was forced winning against best defense.
Finish calculated and played: Qg5+ restricted the king to f7 or h7; ...Kf7 Re7+ forced ...Kf8; Qg7# was supported by Re7 and closed every flight. Final king-flight and queen-support accounting succeeded on this concrete board. Promotion and an active king were useful, but the bishop-defense mistake persists and this easier-opponent win alone does not establish transfer.
Checkpoint: fifteen scored benchmarks, W5 D0 L10. Full strength W0 D0 L6; skill 0 W4 D0 L1; 1800 setting W1 D0 L3. Referee provisional engine-relative Elo 1590, 95% credible interval 1300–1890, nine calibrated games, six uncalibrated full-strength games excluded. Thirty-point increase after this win is small relative to uncertainty; no sustained improvement established. Eight stopped practice sessions remain retained. Illegal totals unchanged: two benchmark submissions and one practice submission, none in games 12–15.
Trial 13 continues to the game-18 checkpoint. Retain the candidate-board inspection, but explicitly check friendly interposers along a queen's defensive line: placing Ng5 removed rather than added the queen's defense of Bh6. For game 16, apply the same short actual-board pass before every pawn capture and minor-piece move; pursue active king/rook coordination when an ending arises, while distinguishing supported promotion from a claim of a forced win. Next fixed-rotation benchmark 16: Black against skill 0. No active benchmark or practice. Goal active toward 200 scored games; 185 remain.

## Benchmark 16 — Black, skill 0, win by checkmate (46 plies)
No illegal submissions. Chose ...Nf6/...e6/...d5, ...Be7 and castling against c4/d4. ...a5 challenged the queenside, ...Nbd7 avoided a b5 attack on a c6 knight, and ...b6/...Bb7 developed the other bishop. ...axb4 Qc2 ...bxa3 gained pawns while the a-file rook supported the advance. ...Nxb6 answered cxb6 with c7 guarding the resulting knight. White later Nxa3 recovered the advanced a-pawn. Bxe5 dxe5 exchanged the central knight; ...Nd7 answered the pawn attack on Nf6, then ...Nxe5 took the undefended pawn. No major piece or unsupported minor was lost in the played continuation.
Useful actual-board checks: ...Nc4 was supported by d5 and Nb6. Bb4 opened b4-c5-d6-e7-f8 against my rook, so ...Re8 saved it. Moving the queen away from d8 then connected Ra8 and Re8 across the back rank; it did not leave either rook undefended. Qh4 g3 Qe4 kept the queen defended by d5 while pressuring the center.
Decisive observed defender change: Rac1 removed the rook from a1, where it had guarded a3. Nxa3 captured White's knight and attacked Qc2. In a Bxa3 continuation the a-file rook could take that bishop without the original a1 rook recapture; that was a calculated conditional line, not the actual reply. White chose Qxc7 instead, taking a pawn and attacking Bb7/Nb6. I chose ...d4 to open b7-c6-d5-e4-f3-g2, transferring Qe4's defense from d5 to Bb7 and threatening the king. After Rxd4, Qg2# was backed by that bishop; the king could not capture the queen or escape, and no interposition could answer the adjacent queen check. The actual win depended on White leaving this mating threat unanswered. I do not claim ...d4 forced immediate mate against every defense.
Local transfer evidence: this game used an enemy defender removal to win material, noticed the rook attack after a bishop move, and updated the bishop line after a pawn move to give a protected mating queen. This is a better fresh safety outcome than games 13–15, but it is one skill-0 game with opponent errors; it does not prove the method reliable or sustained Elo improvement.
Checkpoint: sixteen scored benchmarks, W6 D0 L10. Full strength W0 D0 L6; skill 0 W5 D0 L1; 1800 setting W1 D0 L3. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1610, 95% credible interval 1340–1900, ten calibrated games; six full-strength games excluded. Twenty-point increase after this win remains small relative to uncertainty. Eight stopped practice sessions retained. Illegal totals unchanged: two benchmark submissions and one practice submission; none in games 12–16.
Trial 13 continues through game 18, with the next two fresh tests against the 1800 setting. Retain the explicit vacancy/interposer check; distinguish a protected recapture from a royal capture onto an enemy-controlled square. Target for game 17: preserve development and material while responding to forcing replies, then use coordinated pieces rather than speculative unsupported checks. Next fixed-rotation benchmark 17 is White at the 1800 setting. No active benchmark or practice. Goal active toward 200 scored games; 184 remain.

## Benchmark 17 — White, 1800 setting, loss by resignation (80 plies)
No illegal submissions. Resigned a minor piece and pawn behind after 40...Bb4, with pressure on f2 and no concrete recovery found. This is a practical resignation, not a proof of forced mate. Queens were exchanged after ...Qxf3 Qxf3 Rxf3; Ba1 restored Rb1's defense of b3, Kh2 blocked the passed h-pawn, and Kxh3 recovered it. These local repairs did not recover the lost knight.
Main failure: g2-g4 removed the cheap defender of Nf3 and exposed king squares. ...Nb6 attacked Qc4 while opening Bb7's diagonal to f3. Qe2 answered the queen attack, but ...Qd5 concentrated another attacker on f3. Kg2 supplied a last recapture temporarily while pinning Nf3 to the king. Black's ...Kg8 and ...fxg4 later opened the f-file for Rf8, and the new g4 pawn controlled f3. The earlier king recapture was then impossible. Kg1 removed the absolute pin, but I failed to find a timely complete defense; ...Rf5, Ng3, ...Qxf3 led to the piece loss. Ng3 controlled h1 and attacked Rf5, but the rook recaptured on f3 after the queen exchange. Long hypothetical branches did not produce a reliable actual-board defense.
Transfer assessment: the skill-0 success in game 16 did not transfer to this stronger opponent. The defender-removal and king-line problem persists. For game 18, prefer development and retaining pawn shields; before a shield pawn advances, identify its defended minor and the newly accessible checking squares. Once an enemy line changes, discard the old recapture assumption and inspect the new forcing sequence. Full trial-13 assessment remains after game 18.
Checkpoint: seventeen scored benchmarks, W6 D0 L11. Full strength W0 D0 L6; skill 0 W5 D0 L1; 1800 setting W1 D0 L4. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1580, 95% credible interval 1320–1850; eleven calibrated games, six full-strength games excluded. No sustained improvement established. Eight stopped practice sessions retained. Illegal totals remain two benchmark and one practice, none in games 12–17. No active game or practice. Next benchmark 18: Black versus the 1800 setting. Goal active toward 200 scored games; 183 remain.

## Benchmark 18 — Black, 1800 setting, loss by resignation (63 plies)
No illegal submissions. Resigned after 31...Rb3+ 32.cxb3 lost a whole rook. I considered king escapes and c2-c3 interposition but completely omitted c2xb3, the immediate pawn capture of the checking rook. Check did not protect the destination. This was my own unassisted calculation error, not an infrastructure issue. A full rook deficit was the reason for resignation, without a claim of forced mate.
Earlier observed local successes: developed and castled in the Italian structure; bishop exchanges kept material level. Re8 added e5 defense before Nc6-d4, then Raxd8 preserved that rook defender during the queen exchange. ...cxd4 created a supported central pawn; knight maneuvering and king activity led to a material-level rook ending. ...f6 supplied e5's pawn defender after Ke7 blocked Re8's line. ...Ne6 answered Nd3's attack on Nc5, and later ...Nc5 was supported by Kd6. ...b6 allowed Kxc5 after Nxc5. None of these local decisions establishes an objectively equal or superior ending. ...a5-a4 and ...b5/bxc4+ opened the b-file, but the first rook incursion failed the most elementary destination-capture test.
Trial 13 assessment after three rotations: games 13–18 W2 D0 L4, with both wins against skill 0. Overall eighteen scored, W6 D0 L12. Full strength W0 D0 L6; skill 0 W5 D0 L1; 1800 setting W1 D0 L5. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1560, 95% credible interval 1310–1810; twelve calibrated games and six full-strength games excluded. Point estimate is unchanged from game 12 and below the initial six-game estimate of 1870, with overlapping uncertainty. No demonstrated sustained Elo improvement. Skill-0 success coexists with repeated fresh tactical losses against stronger opponents. The candidate-board method remains unreliable, especially when speculative forcing lines displace a basic capture check.

## Benchmark 19 — White, full strength, loss by resignation (50 plies)
No illegal submissions. Resigned an exchange and two pawns behind after 25...Nxb3, with no concrete recovery found. Practical resignation, not proof of forced mate. The full-strength result is retained and excluded from the nominal rating estimate by the existing referee rules.
Developed in a d4/c4 structure, castled, and recaptured the central pawn exchanges. Qd2 retained d4's queen defender; Rad1 followed by Qe2 freed that rook's line to d4 and supported Bc4. Main failure: after ...Nb4, Bb3 vacated c4 on a6-b5-c4-d3-e2-f1. ...Ba6 attacked Qe2 with Rf1 behind it. Qe3 Bxf1 Rxf1 lost the exchange. I checked immediate attacks on the bishop's new square but missed the line uncovered at its old square. Thus the remembered destination-capture drill did not prevent this different fresh line-opening error.
Second defender-removal error: after Bxe7 Nxe7, Ne5 vacated f3, removing d4's cheap knight defender. ...Nf5 attacked the queen and d4; Qe4 Qxd4 lost the pawn. Qxd4 Nxd4 removed queens. Re1 and later Re2/Re4 supported the remaining knight, and h3 created a flight, but ...Nxb3 took the undefended front b-pawn. The b2 pawn did not guard b3: forward pawn occupancy is not diagonal defense. These are actual errors, not signs of successful transfer.

Trial 15 outcome, practice 10 (skill 5, remembered game-19 position): Bd3 Nfd5 Bxe7 Nxe7 Rfe1 Nxd3 Rxd3 Ba6 Qe3 Nd5. Ten plies, no illegal submissions. Material remained level in the actual continuation, but Rxd3 recreated the skewer: ...Ba6 attacked Rd3 with Qe2 behind it. Qe3 allowed ...Bxd3 Qxd3, which would have cost the exchange. The practice opponent instead chose ...Nd5, attacking the queen and c3 knight; that observed choice left material level at the horizon. Thus my commentary calling the material target failed was premature as a description of the actual count, but the intended safety method did fail to avoid offering the exchange. Survival depended on the opponent not taking it. No claim that ...Bxd3 is best or that a complete defense was proved. Drill stopped unfinished with its last threat retained.
Decision for benchmark 20: retain source-line inspection before choosing a retreat or recapture. A recapture may put a rook in front of its own queen on the same enemy bishop ray. In this remembered line, Qxd3 instead of Rxd3 is my own untested candidate for later study; the rook on f1 had already moved, so that queen would no longer have a rook behind it. Do not report that candidate as a successful drill. Return to fresh benchmark 20 next, Black against full strength; next broader evaluation after game 24.

## Benchmark 20 — Black, full strength, loss by resignation (49 plies)
No illegal submissions. Resigned after 25.Re3 a full minor piece behind with equal pawn counts, an exposed king, and no concrete recovery found. This is a practical resignation, not proof of mate. Kept the result; no engine analysis or external advice used.
Chose ...e5/...Nc6/...a6/...Nf6/...Be7, ...b5/...d6 and castling. Qc7 supported c5 and would guard e5 after d6 vacated by recapturing on c5. Closed center after d5. ...Bd7 developed; ...c4 b4 forced Na5-b7. ...a5 axb5 Bxb5 restored material level. Decisive own-blocker error: Na3 attacked Bb5; I retreated Ba6 without checking that Nb7 blocked all retreats through b7. b5 trapped the bishop. ...Bxb5 Nxb5 recovered a pawn but lost the bishop. The new square was initially unattacked, yet the next pawn attack left no safe exit. Source/destination checking alone again failed to include my own future escape blockers.
The queen retreated Qb6, then Qc5 after Rb1 threatened the opened b-file, then Qc8 after Ba3. The remaining d6 defender was overloaded: Nxe5 dxe5 opened Ba3-b4-c5-d6-e7, allowing Bxe7. ...Re8 saved the attacked f8 rook; Bxf6 gxf6 exchanged a bishop for my other knight and damaged the pawn shield. These exchanges retained the original whole-minor deficit while losing the extra pawn. No claim of adequate compensation. Resigned instead of treating a speculative attack as recovery.

## Benchmark 21 — White, skill 0, loss by resignation (156 plies)
One illegal submission: I attempted e2-e3 after Ke3 had already been played and the opponent had answered ...Ra2. The referee rejected the duplicate without changing the board. I had reasoned from a stale position during extended hypothetical analysis; the actual board then guided Rf5. Total illegal benchmark submissions now three, in games 10, 11 and 21. One earlier practice illegal remains separately recorded. No infrastructure fault or external assistance.
Developed, castled, exchanged a bishop on e6, and opened the center with d4. Qd2 initially retained c2's defense and supported Be3/Nc3. The f/g-pawn advances produced a protected g6 pawn and restricted the king. I avoided an immediate deflection error after ...Rxf5: exf5 would have opened the fourth rank to ...Qxc4, so Qe6 first saved the queen and threatened the unguarded e7 bishop. ...Rff8 Qxe7 won it. This was a useful local actual-board response, not proof the attack was sound.
The material gain was lost by another queen-defender removal: Qh4 defended Bf2 along h4-g3-f2, but Qg4 did not. ...Qxf2+ captured the bishop with Rf8 backing the queen, so the king could not recapture. Later Qxf3 Rxf3 removed queens. The stated queen-source defense method failed on a fresh position. Rook cooperation and king activity then kept many immediate threats under control, but the defended rook battery eventually took g6; recapturing would only exchange rooks while losing the pawn. The final rook-pair exchange occurred with ...Nxg6+ rather than the anticipated rook recapture, preserving f-file pressure and requiring another king move. I should not assume which enemy piece will recapture.
The ending recovered material with Kxg5 and Kxh4 while Black's king was distant. However, ...c4 bxc4 bxc4 created a passed c-pawn. Kg3, Kf3 and Ke2 arrived too late: ...c3, ...c2, ...c1=Q. Black promoted at ply 156. I resigned with three unadvanced pawns against the queen and a pawn; no concrete adequate defense found, without claiming a proved forced mate. Kingside pawn gains did not compensate for the promotion race. Extensive speculative variations neither prevented the bishop error nor kept the latest board consistently in view.
Checkpoint: twenty-one scored, W6 D0 L15. Full strength W0 D0 L8; skill 0 W5 D0 L2; 1800 setting W1 D0 L5. Referee provisional engine-relative Elo 1490, 95% credible interval 1260–1730; thirteen calibrated games, eight full-strength excluded. Seventy-point decline from game 20; uncertainty remains broad. No sustained improvement established. Eleven stopped practice sessions retained. All results and the illegal rejection remain recorded.

Compare only the observed continuation; this alternative is not independently established as best or drawing. Record any pawn loss and whether the original race recurs. After this learning period, return to benchmark 22, Black against skill 0; next broader evaluation after game 24. For fresh play, re-anchor to the latest referee board before each submission, and keep the immediate destination/source checks ahead of remote hypothetical branches. This is a revision prompted by failure, not evidence it has worked.

Trial 17 outcome, practice 12 (skill 5, remembered game-21 pawn ending): Kf4 Kc6 Ke3 Kb7 e5 dxe5 Ke4 c4 b4 Kc6 Ke3 Kc7 Kd2 Kd7 Kc3 Kc6 Kd2 Kd5 Kc3 e4. Twenty plies. No illegal submission or promotion. White finished with three pawns against four, king c3 against d5; Black retains c4/e4 passed-pawn threats. The original immediate promotion race was avoided within this horizon, but e5 sacrificed a pawn and created the second central passer. This additional choice prevents attributing the entire continuation to Kf4 alone. The recorded result supports only short remembered-position survival with a nearer king, not an established drawing defense, best move, or transfer. I did not test beyond twenty plies. Drill stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for fresh benchmark 22: prioritize the current referee board and immediate opponent reply; the duplicate Ke3 submission in game 21 showed board drift despite long analysis. Before relocating a queen, inspect the pieces it actually defended, including diagonal support to a bishop. For pawn endings, count the opposing pawn's forward route and the king's legal approach before collecting a wing pawn; a profitable capture can leave the king too far from a promotion square. ## Benchmark 22 — Black, skill 0, win by checkmate (74 plies)
One illegal submission at move 34: Re8-e2 was blocked by my own e5 pawn. Corrected with Rd8. Total illegal benchmark submissions now four (games 10, 11, 21, 22), plus one practice submission. The actual-board method remains unreliable; this was not an infrastructure fault.
Developed and castled in a d5/e6 structure. Qc7 removed the queen pin on Nf6 and defended Bd6; e5 answered Bf4's attack on the queen. Na5/Nc4 escaped b5 while using queen and pawn support. Bxf3/Bxf3 followed by Nxd2 removed both white knights. White Bh1 declined Bxd2, so Nc4 saved the attacked knight. Bxd5 allowed Nf6xd5; Qxc4 then lost the white queen to Qc7xc4. Qxc5 collected the undefended remaining bishop. These opponent errors supplied a decisive material lead, not evidence of a sound forced attack.
Conversion was wasteful: Bg5, c4, Bxc1, cxd5 exchanged my knight for a rook; Be3 then Qd4 allowed fxe3, giving back the bishop for a pawn after Qxe3+. I noticed that Rf2's interposition made Qxf2+ unsafe because the king could recapture, and brought the a8 rook to c8 instead. The passed d-pawn reached d7; Rcd8 allowed dxe8=Q+, but Rxe8 immediately recaptured the promoted queen. Rxf7 left the last white rook available to Kxf7. The illegal rook move followed despite these local checks. Final coordination was concrete: Qxe2 controlled the second rank and supported d1, then Rd1# left no king escape or block.
Checkpoint: twenty-two scored benchmarks, W7 D0 L15. Full strength W0 D0 L8; skill 0 W6 D0 L2; 1800 setting W1 D0 L5. In the final position, Qe2 guarded d1 diagonally and g2/h2 along the second rank; Rd1 supplied the first-rank check. This illustrates actual complementary control rather than unsupported repeated checks. ## Benchmark 23 — White, 1800 setting, loss by resignation (50 plies)
No illegal submissions. Practical resignation a bishop and pawn behind after 25...O-O, without a concrete recovery found or a proof of forced loss. Main failure occurred at 7.Nbd2: it interposed on Qd1-d2-d3, removing Bd3's only recapture. I incorrectly carried the earlier queen defense forward. After ...e6, c4 Bxd3 could not be answered by Qxd3 because my own Nd2 blocked that path. Re1 saved Rf1 from the bishop's attack, but ...Bxc4 took the c-pawn as well. This is failed transfer of the actual-path/defender method in a fresh opening.
Subsequent local checks avoided another material loss: Ne5 attacked Bc4 with d4/f4 support; a4 and b3 gained bishop tempi; Qc2 connected the rooks, and Red1 removed the rook behind the bishop's line before Ndf3. e4 Nxe4 Nxe4 dxe4 Qxe4 exchanged a knight pair and central pawns with the queen's recapture path actually clear. Bg3 vacated f4 while retaining pawn support; Qf3 threatened f7 with Ne5's support, but castling defended it. These repairs did not recover the opening deficit. Own calculation only; no external study, advice or automated analysis.
Checkpoint: twenty-three scored, W7 D0 L16. Full strength W0 D0 L8; skill 0 W6 D0 L2; 1800 setting W1 D0 L6. ## Benchmark 24 — Black, 1800 setting, loss by resignation (65 plies)
No illegal submissions. Resigned after 32...Qg7 33.Bf6, while still a pawn ahead in actual material. The queen remained on g7, pinned to Kh8 by Bf6; I did not find an adequate defense against the queen captures and king threats. No queen capture, material sacrifice or mate occurred in the recorded game after Bf6. This was a practical assessment and resignation, not proof of a forced loss or mate.
Vienna-like opening: ...d5/exd5/Nxd5/fxe5 left a pawn deficit. ...Nc6 and ...Bc5 developed; ...Be6 supplied a cheaper defender of Nd5 rather than relying on Qd8 alone against two minor attackers. Castling, ...Bb6, ...Qd7, ...Ndb4 and ...axb6 kept material losses to pawn structure changes. ...Bf5 supported Nd3; Nxb2 gained the b-pawn and Na4 used Ra8's support against Bb3. This recovered the pawn deficit. Ra8 then remained tied to Na4's defense; I repeatedly considered moving it and rejected the resulting undefended knight.
Main strategic/tactical failure: ...f6 exf6 gxf6 removed both f/g shield pawns from their original squares. ...Be4 answered Nh4 but c5+ vacated c4 and opened Bb3-c4-d5-e6-f7-g8, forcing Kg7. Bh6+ forced Kh8. ...f5 blocked Qf4's route to f6 and gave Be4 pawn support. ...h5 created h7 as a king flight, then Kh7 used it, but Rc7 later controlled the seventh rank again. The cheaper pawn/king controls were locally checked without solving the whole king net.
Local material success: ...Nxd4 captured the d-pawn; cxb6 cxb6 then Nxd4 Qxd4 exchanged the knight pair and kept a pawn advantage. Be4 interposed between Qf4 and Qd4, so the queen recapture was not immediately exposed to the other queen. Rc7+ Kh8 followed. Qb2 threatened a bishop-backed capture on g2; Rd2 supplied a rook defender and attacked the queen. Qa1 maintained the long diagonal to f6/h8 rather than dropping it. Rd6 introduced pressure along the sixth rank, and Qg7 was chosen under that attack. Bf6 then pinned the queen and supported g7, undermining the imagined king recapture after a rook capture there. The evolving bishop support and interpositions were not kept reliable in my extensive speculative analysis. I should not label a defended queen safe merely because the king was an earlier recapturer.
Trial 18/19 transfer assessment: no illegal submissions in games 23–24, but game 23 repeated a defender obstruction and game 24 failed whole-king safety. Practice 13's remembered Qe2-d3 recapture succeeded narrowly; it did not demonstrate transfer to the Black opening or fix king nets. Long hypothetical branches again consumed substantial wall time while confusing rook check geometry, source/destination blockers and the changing support for captures. The method is not established as reliable.

## Benchmark 25 — White, full strength, loss by resignation (64 plies)
One illegal submission: after Nxc6 Qxa1, I attempted c6-a8. That is a two-file/two-rank diagonal, not a knight move. The referee rejected it, and Nd4 was the correction. I had falsely treated Nc6 as attacking Ra8 throughout extended hypothetical analysis. The exchange sequence therefore rested on a basic geometry error: Nxc6 took a knight, but ...Qxa1 took the rook without a corresponding attack on Ra8. This was my own error, with no external assistance or infrastructure fault. Total illegal benchmark submissions now five (10, 11, 21, 22, 25), plus one practice submission.
Early local changes: Be2 kept a real diagonal queen defender while Nd2 later developed; b3 answered ...Qb6 without abandoning a knight on c3, which was still on b1. Castled with f2/g2 intact. Bg5/Bh4/Bg3 answered pawn and knight attacks. Ne5 removed d4's cheap knight defender; Nxg3 hxg3 exchanged bishop and knight, keeping f2/g2 while moving the h-pawn. ...Qxd4 then won the d-pawn. Nxc6 was chosen with the erroneous a8-rook idea; ...Qxa1 and the illegal attempted recapture exposed that failure. Qd3 subsequently guarded Nd4 without a knight on d2 interposing. ...Qxa2 took another pawn, and Bxg4 recovered the advanced g-pawn before it acquired another defender.
Later observations: c4/cxd5 temporarily recovered a pawn, but ...exd5 later removed it. Nf3 vacated d4; Nc4 interposed against ...Bb5's queen/rook line and attacked Qa5. Nxa5 Bxd3 exchanged queens. Re1 saved the rook from the bishop's diagonal, and b4 freed the knight's b3 retreat before ...Rxe1+ Nxe1 exchanged the last white rook for the e8 rook. Nc2 answered ...Bc3's attack on Ne1 and defended b4; Nc5, ...d3, Ne3 then saved that knight and controlled d1. ...Bxb4 won b4 after Nc2 left its defense, also attacking Nc5, so Ne4 evacuated. Nf6 was legal and un-attacked on arrival. I resigned after ...a4 with no adequate overall plan found against the central and a-file passers. Actual ending: White Kg1, knights e3/f6, Bg4, pawns f2/g2/g3; Black Kb8, Rd8, bishops b4/b5, pawns a4/b7/d3/f7/h6. An exchange and two pawns behind. No promotion or forced mate was demonstrated; practical resignation.
Transfer: the f2/g2 shield remained intact and there was no king-net loss in this game. That is a local observation, not established Elo improvement. Geometry and recapture errors still caused the decisive material deficit. Extremely long speculative variations again proved unreliable; a long imagined line cannot substitute for verifying whether the first claimed knight attack even exists. No sustained improvement established. Twenty-five scored, W7 D0 L18; full strength W0 D0 L9, skill 0 W6 D0 L2, 1800 setting W1 D0 L7. Elo unchanged at 1490 (1270–1700), sixteen calibrated and nine excluded full-strength games. Fifteen unfinished practice sessions retained.

Trial 21: self-study of the knight error using my own coordinates: a legal knight displacement changes one file and two ranks, or two files and one rank. c6-a8 changes two of each, so it cannot be a knight move or attack. Verify this before any knight-fork narrative. Then test my own alternative Qxd4 from game 25, ply 26, rather than the erroneous Nxc6/Ra8 plan. Ten recorded plies against skill 5; observable target is a legal queen exchange and material preservation, with special attention to the bishop/ c2 fork after ...Nxd4. My candidate Bd3 moves the attacked bishop and gives c2 a bishop recapture. This is a remembered-position test, not proof of best play or equality. Return afterward to benchmark 26 (Black, full strength); broader checkpoint remains after game 30. No external study or advice, automated search/scoring/analysis or move selection, and no measured token counter submitted.

Trial 21 outcome, practice 16: Qxd4 Nxd4 Bd3 Bg7 Nxg4 h5 Ne3 Nxb3 axb3 Bxa1. Ten plies, no illegal submission. The queen exchange was legal, Bd3 saved the attacked bishop and supplied c2's recapture, and Nxg4 recovered the g-pawn before Ne3 answered h5. However, the overall material target failed: ...Nxb3 vacated d4 on g7-f6-e5-d4-c3-b2-a1, while also attacking Ra1 by a real knight move. axb3 captured that knight but allowed ...Bxa1. Nb1 still blocked Rf1's horizontal path to a1; the bishop was not immediately recapturable. Final actual deficit: an exchange and one pawn. No proof that the loss was unavoidable or that Qxd4 was best. This was a remembered test against skill 5, not transfer, and it shows that fixing one mistaken knight attack did not fix the later revealed rook attack. Drill stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for fresh benchmark 26: keep the explicit two-by-one knight displacement check before fork claims, and evaluate the first actual opponent capture/interposition before writing a long remote branch. The source vacancy of the enemy knight mattered as much as its new destination. Do not count a rook recapture through an own knight. The longer-analysis method is still ineffective; this trial does not justify confidence in it. Next fixed-rotation game 26 is Black versus full strength. Next broader assessment remains after benchmark 30.

## Benchmark 26 — Black, full strength, loss by resignation (43 plies)
No illegal submissions. Resigned after 20...g6 21.fxg6 hxg6 22.Rxf6 won my undefended knight. Actual deficit a knight and pawn; no concrete recovery found, without a claim of forced mate. Full-strength result retained and excluded from the nominal rating estimate. Twenty-six scored, W7 D0 L19; full W0 D0 L10, skill 0 W6 D0 L2, 1800 setting W1 D0 L7. Elo unchanged at 1490 (1270–1700), sixteen calibrated and ten excluded games.
Developed and castled in the Ruy-like structure. Bxc6 dxc6 Nxe5 lost the e-pawn. Qd4 attacked Ne5, but Nf3 both retreated and attacked the queen, so Qb4 was required before any supposed capture on e4. Be6 developed; Rae8 supplied a real defender for Be7 before Ba3 Qa5 Bxe7 Rxe7. Qd2 attacked Qa5, and Qxd2 Nfxd2 exchanged queens. c5/c6 cleared the seventh rank for Re7 to defend b7 and retained a c6 pawn controlling d5. Rfe8 connected the rooks, Kf8 gave Re7 king support, and Rd8 activated the d-file. I did not count those rooks as defending e4 or e5 through an own bishop.
Main failure: f5 attacked Be6; Bc8 was a safe retreat. After a4 I chose ...g6 to remove f5's restriction. The g7 pawn had been Nf6's cheap defender. Moving it removed that defender, and fxg6/hxg6 removed White's f5 pawn from the rook's path. Rf1 then had a clear file through f2/f3/f4/f5 to Nf6. Keeping f7 blocked a rook attack on Kf8, but it did not protect the knight on f6. Kf8 was two ranks away and could not recapture Rf6; the rook on e7 could not recapture diagonally. This is another fresh source-defense and interposer-removal failure. The commentary said I would reassess the knight's defense, but the actual recapture did not apply that reassessment correctly.
Transfer assessment: legal move geometry avoided another rejection, but whole-position defense still failed. The king retained a blocker, while the adjacent minor did not. Long speculative analysis again did not prevent an immediate simple capture. No sustained improvement established. Illegal totals remain five benchmark and one practice; sixteen stopped practice sessions retained.

## Benchmark 27 — White, skill 0, win by checkmate (71 plies)
No illegal submissions. Twenty-seven scored, W8 D0 L19. Full strength W0 D0 L10; skill 0 W7 D0 L2; 1800 setting W1 D0 L7. Referee provisional engine-relative Elo 1510, 95% credible interval 1300–1720; seventeen calibrated games, ten full-strength excluded. Seventeen stopped practice sessions retained. Illegal totals remain five benchmark and one practice. No sustained improvement established.
In the Sicilian, Black Rxc3 bxc3 gave up a rook for a knight, then gxh5 took the other knight. hxg6 en passant removed the two-step g-pawn and opened the queen check from b6 to g1. Be3 interposed, and Qxe3+ Qxe3 gave up Black's queen for the bishop. Those opponent errors supplied the large lead; no claim of a sound forced attack. Nd4 Bxd4+ cxd4 exchanged the remaining knight and bishop. Rxg6+ Kf1 Bb5 pinned Qd3 to Kf1. c4 blocked b5-c4-d3-e2-f1, with Qxc4 available against Bxc4+. Rf6+ Kg1 Rg6+ Kh1 Bc6+ then d5 exd5 cxd5 kept a pawn blocker on the bishop diagonal. Rg3 allowed Qxg3 without recapture. Re1+ Kf8 Qd6+ Kg7 Qxd7 removed the last bishop. Rg1+ Kf8 Qd8# coordinated queen and rook: the queen controlled e8/e7 and checked along rank eight; Rg1 covered g8/g7; f7 was occupied by Black's own pawn.
The final phase preserved the lead without another illegal move. However, speculative branches earlier in the game still produced imagined geometry and flight squares in reasoning; those were corrected before submission, not evidence of a reliable general method. The win depends heavily on actual opponent blunders. No external sources, delegated advice, automated analysis/search/scoring/move selection or measured token counter used.

Trial 23: between-game self-study of game 27's pin and mate, using my own remembered board only. A defended queen is still an expensive loss if attacked by a bishop: after Bb5, merely moving Kf1 would permit Bxd3; c4 instead interposed a cheap pawn. In the final net Qd8 covered e7/e8, but the rook on g1 was needed to cover g7/g8. A queen check alone should not be labeled mate before accounting for all adjacent flights and capture/block possibilities. Fresh-play test for benchmark 28: inspect direct cheap attacks on my queen before each move and verify actual rook/queen coverage of king flights before a mating claim. Next evaluation after game 28, broader paired-rotation assessment after game 30. This study supplies a method hypothesis, not proof of transfer. Return to fixed rotation: Black against skill 0.

## Benchmark 28 — Black, skill 0, win by checkmate (50 plies)
No illegal submissions. Twenty-eight scored, W9 D0 L19. Full strength W0 D0 L10; skill 0 W8 D0 L2; 1800 setting W1 D0 L7. Developed Nf6/Bf5/e6/Bd6 and castled. Bxd6 Qxd6 exchanged bishops. Rae8 and Be4 supplied real support before e5/exd4. After Nxd4 Nxd4 Nxe4, White's remaining knight attacked Qd6; Nf6xe4 removed it. White a3 declined cxd4, so Nc6 retained the exposed knight. Ba6 allowed bxa6, giving two knights against no minors. These opponent errors supplied the material lead.
Fresh defender-removal failure: Qd6-g6 abandoned d5's defense after e6 had already advanced and exchanged. Qxd5 won a pawn and attacked Nc6, whose b7 pawn defender had moved to a6. Nc6-e5 escaped that attack. My reasoning counted Re8 as still defending Ne4, but Ne5 now blocked that rook route. The actual remaining defender was Qg6 along f5-e4; Qxe4 Qxe4 captured White's queen. The recapture was legal and safe, but this does not validate the mistaken rook-defense claim. Another opponent blunder rescued the careless pawn concession.
Nf3+ gxf3 Qxf3 deliberately gave back the remaining knight to open the king cover. Kh2 Re4 Ra1 Rh4 b4 Qxh3+ Kg1 Qh1# completed a supported queen/rook net. I checked the queen's h1 support along h4-h3-h2-h1, including the source vacancy at h3, and the king's flights: g2/h2 controlled by Qh1, h1 protected by Rh4, f1 occupied by White's rook and f2 by White's pawn. ## Benchmark 29 — White, 1800 setting, draw by automatic threefold repetition (127 plies)
No illegal submissions. Twenty-nine scored, W9 D1 L19. Full strength W0 D0 L10; skill 0 W8 D0 L2; 1800 setting W1 D1 L7. Referee provisional engine-relative Elo 1540, 95% credible interval 1340–1740; nineteen calibrated and ten full-strength excluded. Seventeen stopped practice sessions retained. Illegal totals remain five benchmark and one practice.
Italian development and castling, then d4/dxe5 against Black's g5/f6/g4 advances. Nd4 Nxd4 Qxd4 dxe5 Qxd8+ Kxd8 exchanged knights and queens without loss. Nc3/Be3/Rad1 developed. Rfe1 provided a real alternative defender for Be3 before f3 gxf3 gxf3. Bb3 retreated from b5. Re2 vacated the bishop's b4-c3-d2-e1 line before bxa4 Nxa4; c3 gained a bishop tempo but removed Bb3's pawn guard, which I noticed. Nc5/Nxd7/Nxd7 and Be6/Nc5/Bxc5/Bxc5+ reached a materially level rook and opposite-bishop ending. Kg2, Rd7, Bf5 retained rook support through f5-e6-d7; Rxa7 Bxa7 exchanged one rook pair.
Main fresh error: Kg3 h4+ Kg4 Bg1 h3 Rh8 Rg2 allowed Rg8+. My own king on g4 had been an interposer between the checking rook and the undefended rook on g2. Any king departure opened the rook capture. Kxh4 took a pawn while resolving check, but Rxg2 lost the whole rook with no recapture. The direct-attack method missed an enemy checking move that unmasked a second target on the same file. Trial 24 source/interposer tracking failed on this fresh position, despite earlier successful defense replacements.
I continued rather than resigning immediately. Kh5/h4, b4, Bd7/Bxc6/Bxa4 recovered queen-side pawns and removed Black's passer. c4/b5/b6, Bc6/b7/Bd5 and c5/c6 established connected advanced passers. Black's dark bishop blockaded their next squares. Kh6/h5/Kh7/h6 and ...Rxf3 let Kg7 cross the vacated g-file. h7 Rg1+ Kh6 Rg2 h8=Q Rh2+ Kg7 Rxh8 Kxh8 traded the promoted queen for the remaining rook. Keeping Kg7 close enough to recapture on h8 mattered; Kg6 would not have supplied that recapture. Opposite-colored bishops remained, White b7/c6/e4 against Black e5/f6. Kxf6 removed another pawn once Black's king left its defense. Ke6/Kf7 tested entry, but the bishop diagonal guarded d6/e5 and Black's king restricted penetration. The referee ended the game as threefold repetition. I have not independently proved a fortress or best play, and I do not claim a win was impossible.
Assessment: first draw of the experiment and a fresh practical recovery against the 1800 setting. This is evidence that continuing with concrete passed-pawn counterplay can salvage a particular game after a major error; it does not erase the error or establish sustained improvement. The exact final claim mechanism is the referee's recorded automatic repetition result; no manual adjudication or reset. No illegal submissions, external sources/advice or automated chess analysis used; all results retained, no measured token counter submitted.

Rg5 cut off g3/g4 and h5; g5 itself was protected by f6, h3 was occupied by my pawn, and Bf2 checked h4 through g3. Be6 retained Rd7's bishop support while ignoring the king's immediate mating net. This is remembered-position evidence of another failure, not transfer or an infrastructure fault. Practice result retained separately: seventeen stopped unfinished sessions plus one scored practice loss, eighteen sessions total.

If Rg5 instead takes Bf5, e4xf5 is available while the rook remains away from the checking file and king-capture contact. Test ten plies against skill 5, White.
