Trial 26 outcome, practice 19: Rd2 Bb6 Be6 Be3 Re2 Bc1 b3 Bf4 Bg4 axb3 Rb2 Rg6 Rxb3 Kd8. Planned ten plies extended to fourteen to resolve the passed b-pawn. One illegal submission after axb3: c3-b3 was sideways, not a legal pawn capture. Corrected with Rb2, then Rxb3 after the opponent reply. The error is mine, retained in practice; illegal practice total now two (8 and 19), benchmark total remains five. My earlier proposed cxb3 recapture in reasoning was also geometrically wrong; do not count that as a valid defense.
Final material: White rook, bishop and four pawns versus Black rook, bishop and three pawns; original one-pawn advantage retained, rook not lost. No mate. Rd2 kept an actual route through e2 to a prospective checking bishop on f2. Be3 attacked the rook, so Re2 maintained the f2 capture route while escaping. Bf4 threatened Bg3 mate with the rook on g5 still fencing the king. Bg4 interposed on the g-file and was defended by f3 and Kh4; after Bg3+, the rook's defense of g3 would be blocked, permitting Kxg3. Rook capture on g4 instead would allow Kxg4. Black chose axb3, and the rook blockaded and recaptured that pawn after the illegal correction. The concrete royal-threat defense worked over this remembered horizon, while elementary pawn geometry failed. Not proved best, equal or transferred. Drill stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for fresh benchmark 30: rank an immediate checking/mating threat before saving an attacked bishop. If an enemy slider checks through my king's square, inspect targets beyond the king after its departure; a defended king does not defend a rook two ranks away. For every pawn capture, the destination must be one file diagonal and one rank forward; same-rank neighboring pawns cannot capture each other. Retain the concrete recapture-route check from the repeated drill without claiming reliability. Return to fixed rotation, Black against the 1800 setting; assess the full six-game rotation after 30. No additional remembered drill before that evaluation.

## Benchmark 30 — Black, 1800 setting, win by checkmate (72 plies)
One illegal submission at move 8: Nc6-d5 is a one-by-one diagonal, not a knight move. Corrected Bxd5. Six illegal benchmark submissions now (10, 11, 21, 22, 25, 30), plus two practice submissions (8, 19). No forfeits. This is an elementary geometry failure despite previously stating the two-by-one rule; long analysis did not reliably apply it.
Against c4/e4/Qe2, developed e5/Nc6/Nf6/Bc5, castled, d6/Be6. Nd5 tempted the impossible Nc6 capture. Bxd5 exd5 exchanged bishop for knight, and Nc6-e7 saved the knight from the pawn attack. c6/dxc6/bxc6 exchanged the advanced pawn. d5 supplied central play; g5 attacked Nf6, and Nd7 both escaped and defended e5/Bc5. Re8 cleared f8 and Nf5 developed. Bxc5 Nxc5 exchanged the remaining bishops. White castled long. Qa5 attacked the undefended a2 pawn and supported Nc5, but Bh3 attacked Nf5. Nd4, defended by e5, attacked Qe2; Nxd4 exd4 exchanged the knight pair and opened the e-file. Qc2 then allowed Qxa2. White g6 left the king-side defenders unchanged and permitted the concrete forcing attack: Nb3+ Qxb3 Qxb3. The queen on a2 supported b3; a2's pawn was gone, the king's flights were blocked/controlled, and the queen recapture was safe. This won the queen for the last knight.
Be6 offered the remaining white bishop; f7xe6 captured the piece that pinned f7 to Kg8, resolving that pin rather than opening an unchecked diagonal. gxh7+ Kxh7 was legal because White's own h4 pawn blocked Rh1's support of h7. Rab8 supplied b-file support. Rxg7+ lost White's rook to Kxg7. Rd2 Qxb2+ Rxb2 exchanged my queen for that remaining rook; Rxb2 allowed Kxb2 in my assessment, but White instead played f3. I saved the exposed rook with Rb6, defended by a7. This opponent choice retained both rooks. Rh8, c5, Rb5, Kc1, Rxh4 removed the wing pawn. Rh2 cut the second rank; Rbb2 was supported while Rh2 stayed, but would become capturable once the h-rook moved. Rbg2 placed the first rook beyond the king's reach, preserving the second-rank cutoff after Rh1#. Referee confirmed mate.
Limits: large opponent errors supplied most of the material lead. My reasoning initially overlooked that Rd2 could capture Qb2 and imagined earlier rook mates without consistently preserving support; those claims were corrected on the actual board before final submission. The final checks were legal and confirmed, but the opening knight rejection and support mistakes mean the method remains unreliable. This win is fresh 1800-setting evidence, not proof of sustained improvement. All moves my own, no outside sources/advice or automated analysis, no measured token counter.

Methods retained cautiously: actual recapture-route checks, king-source vacancies, cheapest defenders, and king-flight verification. Remembered drills sometimes avoid one error while creating another; their success has not established transfer by itself.

Trial 27 outcome, practice 20: Ne7 Nxf6+ gxf6 Bh6 Re8 Be3 Bxe3 fxe3 Nc6 g4. Ten plies, no illegal submission. One knight pair and one bishop pair exchanged. After Nxf6+, g7-f6 was a legal pawn capture of the checking knight; Qd8-f6 was blocked by my own Ne7, so that queen recapture could not be assumed. Bh6 attacked Rf8, and Re8 escaped; f8 was then still bishop-controlled until Bh6 moved to e3. Bxe3 fxe3 exchanged bishops without a claim of best play.
Limits: remembered position, skill-5 replies, only ten plies. Black now has f6/f7 doubled pawns and an open g-file in front of Kg8; White's f-pawn moved to e3 and its king is still central. g4 has just advanced. Those structural and king-safety consequences were not tested further. The drill avoided the impossible Nc6-d5 move, but the actual benchmark had already shown that merely remembering the displacement rule did not ensure applying it. This horizon supports legal geometry and material retention here, not sustained transfer, soundness or equality. Practice stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for fresh benchmark 31: re-anchor the actual board before each choice, verify knight displacement before imagining a capture, and keep direct checking threats ahead of a long speculative continuation. When a defender moves, distinguish its protection of a bishop from that bishop's protection of a rook; saving the first piece can still leave another exposed. Before declaring a rook mate, verify both rooks' safety after the checking rook moves, including adjacent king captures of the other rook. Next fixed game is White versus full strength; next broader evaluation after 36. No extra trial before returning to the benchmark.

## Benchmark 31 — White, full strength, loss by resignation (42 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-one scored, W10 D1 L20; full strength W0 D0 L11. Calibrated results remain skill 0 W8 D0 L2 and 1800 W2 D1 L7; Elo remains 1590 (1390–1780), twenty calibrated and eleven excluded. Practical resignation with an exchange and three pawns down, no adequate material-preserving continuation found; not a proved forced mate or unavoidable loss. All records retained.
Italian e4/Nf3/Bc4/castle/d3/c3/Re1/Nbd2. Ng4 added a second attack on f2. I chose d4, citing the bishop-line interposition and Nd2's replacement defenses for Bc4/e4. However, exd4 cxd4 Nxd4 Nxd4 Bxd4 exploited the Nd2 blocker on Qd1's recapture route. Ndf3 opened that route and attacked Bd4, but Bxf2+ Kh1 Bxe1 Qxe1 took f2 and an exchange. Local defense-replacement checks did not adequately account for the whole forcing sequence. This is the recurring Italian central capture/blocked queen recapture and f2-pressure problem, not a successful fresh application of the previous method.
Bg5 attacked Qe7, but Qxe4 took the undefended-by-a-cheap-piece pawn and offered a knight-fork queen trade: Qxe4 would permit Nf2+ against Kh1 and Qe4. Qe2 instead let Bc4 and the queen defend each other; Qxe2 Bxe2 exchanged queens. Re8 attacked Be2, and Rae1 supplied a rook defender. Ne5/Bf4/Nxf3/gxf3 exchanged the remaining knight pair. The pawn recapture retained Be2 as an e-file interposer and opened g2; Bxf3 would have exposed Re1 after its knight defender disappeared. Be6 attacked a2, so a3 saved that pawn. Bc4 then put a second attacker on Be2 while Re8 already covered the same file. Moving Be2 would expose the undefended Re1; Re1xe2 after a bishop capture would put the rook on an unrecapturable capture square. I could not preserve sufficient material or find useful counterplay and resigned.
Assessment: some immediate pin/fork/interposer checks were correctly applied without an illegal submission, but the early forcing sequence still produced a large deficit. Long speculative branches again consumed attention without preventing the key tactical losses. The improved 25–30 block has not transferred to full-strength survival; all eleven such games remain losses. No sustained improvement established. No outside sources/advice/delegation, no automated chess search/scoring/analysis/move selection, no measured token counter.

Trial 28: remembered game-31 position at ply 16, after 8...Ng4. Test my alternative h2-h3 instead of d3-d4, against skill 5 as White for ten plies. If Nxf2 forks Qd1, consider Qc2, whose route through c2 is clear after c3; use the actual reply, not the speculative continuation. Observable target: avoid the original central pawn loss and Bxf2/Bxe1 exchange loss across the horizon, and record any pawn sacrifice or royal threat. In particular, a knight leaving f2 can uncover Bc5's g1 diagonal, so do not treat a capture of the knight as automatically resolving a double check. This is my unverified alternative, not an established best move or equal position. Return afterward to benchmark 32, Black full strength; next broader evaluation remains after 36.

The original central loss sequence did not occur within this horizon, but Black did not choose the hypothesized Nxf2: Bxf2+ still required a king move, and Ne3 attacked Qd1/Bc4. Qb3 escaped with a2 support; a4 attacked that queen by pawn. Qa3 was b2-supported but boxed by my a2/b2/c3 pawns and Black's a4 pawn, Nc6 (attacking b4) and d6 pawn (attacking c5). Nc2 then forked Qa3/Ra1/Re1. No safe queen exit found by my reasoning; no queen was actually captured and no forced-loss proof was established. The intended whole-position retention method failed through the new queen trap, not through an illegal submission or infrastructure fault. Drill stopped unfinished and retained.

Trial 29 outcome, practice 22: Qe2 Bxe1 Nxe1 Nxc4 dxc4 a4 Ndf3 a3 bxa3 f6. Ten plies, no illegal submission. Queen retained on e2; no a3 queen trap. White finishes with queen, rook, bishop, two knights and seven pawns against queen, two rooks, bishop, knight and seven pawns: an exchange down, pawns equal. The rook loss still occurred; this alternative did not save the original exchange. Nxe1 recaptured the bishop with legal one-file/two-rank geometry, and dxc4 recaptured the knight with legal one-file/one-rank pawn geometry. Nd2-f3 freed c1, added an actual knight defender to Ne1, but removed Nd2's e4 guard; Qe2 still supported e4 through the now-vacated e3 square. b2xa3 removed Black's passed pawn and opened Bc1-b2-a3 as its recapturing pawn's defender. My queen's file remained clear without moving into the trapped edge position.
Limits: remembered position and skill-5 replies, only ten plies. Black's f6 has just advanced; doubled a/c pawns and the exchange deficit remain. No proof of equality, best play or transfer. The original h3 trial failed through a queen/rook fork; this repeat avoided that fork over the chosen horizon but still paid an exchange. Practice stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for benchmark 32: preserve the actual defender routes and queen exits after every reply, rather than treating a pawn-defended queen as automatically safe. A defended queen can still be taken profitably by a cheaper piece, and a supported edge square can have no safe exit after the next pawn or knight move. Keep slider path checks concrete; a knight moves two-by-one, a pawn capture one-by-one forward. Do not rely on Nd2 for a queen recapture through d2 or on an e2 bishop's continued presence after it moves. Return now to fixed benchmark 32, Black full strength; broader assessment after 36. No further remembered drill before that benchmark.

## Benchmark 32 — Black, full strength, loss by resignation (47 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-two scored, W10 D1 L21; full strength W0 D0 L12. Calibrated results unchanged: skill 0 W8 D0 L2, 1800 setting W2 D1 L7. Provisional Elo remains 1590 (1390–1780), twenty calibrated and twelve full-strength excluded. Resigned after Qxd7+: actual material two rooks and seven pawns against queen, rook, knight and six pawns. Kf8 was a surviving check response in my reasoning, but no realistic recovery found. Not a proof of forced mate after that response; practical resignation retained.
Tried a c6/d5 structure instead of the previous e5/French structures. e5/Bf5/h4/h5, Bd3/Bxd3/Qxd3 and e6 gave matching bishop trades. Nh6/Bxh6/gxh6 exchanged knight for bishop and left doubled h-pawns/open g-file. Be7/Nd7/Qc7/castle long developed without material loss. White g3/Nbd2/Ne1/Ng2 prepared knight activity. c5/c3 and Nf8 kept an actual additional e6 defender before any contemplated f-pawn move. b4/cxd4/cxd4 exchanged the central pawn pair. I noticed that c3's departure removed b4's cheap defender and took Bxb4.
Main failure: Rfc1 attacked Qc7 and pinned it absolutely to Kc8 on the newly opened c-file. I had considered the queen's support by the king but failed to apply the royal source-vacancy restriction before capturing the b-pawn. Qc7 could not retreat sideways while Kc8 remained on the file. The pawn gain did not justify the royal/queen configuration. Bxd2/Qxd2 traded my bishop for a knight, then Qxc1+/Rxc1+ gave the queen for a rook. Kd7 centralized the king and retained a cheap e6 guard, Rb8 protected b7, and Kd8 prepared an interposition against Qd6+. Qd6+/Nf8-d7 worked as a legal check block and supplied Nd7's guard of Rb8.
Second failure: Rc7 added a second attacker to the pinned Nd7. While Kd8 remained, Qxd7 would be mate: Rc7 supported Qd7 and the queen/rook covered the king's adjacent squares. Ke8 avoided that immediate mate but allowed Qxd7+, losing the knight. A royal defender could not capture the checking queen because the enemy rook defended its destination. The actual checker/pinner and source vacancies mattered more than the listed number of defenders. I resigned with the large deficit above.
Assessment: legal geometry was applied without a rejection, and early pawn/defender checks prevented some immediate losses. Whole-position royal line safety failed after a tempting pawn gain, then the nominal defense of a pinned knight failed against two coordinated attackers. Long speculative analysis was again ineffective and repeatedly miscounted piece guards or conditional paths before correction. The method still has no sustained transfer to full strength; all twelve such games lost. No external sources, advice, delegation or automated chess search/scoring/analysis/move-selection code used; no measured token counter submitted.

Qe7 is not available while Be7 occupies it; do not assume a queen exit through an own piece. The pawn on b4 remains a possible gain, but royal safety comes first. The king's move off c8 made Qc7-d7 legal when Rac1 attacked the queen, preventing the original absolute c-file pin and queen-for-rook concession. a3 supplied b4 a pawn defender, so Bxb4 was no longer the measured free-pawn capture. Ng6 was defended by f7; moving f7 would remove that knight guard as well as e6's pawn guard. Bf8 vacated e7, and Qe7 retained e6 support and d8's queen defense along e7-d8. The queen remained safe through the observed horizon.
Limits: remembered position, skill 5 and ten plies. White's Rc3 has just changed the queen/knight support routes, and Black's doubled h-pawns/passive bishop remain. Not proved equal, best or transferred. I did not continue the position. The earlier full-strength failure came after declining this king move to collect b4; this drill establishes only one local successful alternative against different replies. Decision for benchmark 33: check royal line openings before a pawn gain and distinguish a defended queen from a queen that may legally escape. A king behind its queen can make a rook attack decisive even though the king nominally defends the queen. Count both actual attackers on a pinned knight; a king cannot recapture a checking queen protected by a rook. Keep calculation tied to the current reply: the long remote branches in game 32 remained ineffective and did not prevent the central error. Return to fixed rotation, White skill 0, benchmark 33. Next broader evaluation after 36. No extra remembered drill before returning to measured play.

## Benchmark 33 — White, skill 0, draw by automatic threefold repetition (111 plies)
One illegal submission: g5-g6 would vacate the interposer between Qb5 and Kh5, exposing the king along rank five. Corrected Kh4. Seven benchmark illegal submissions now; practice remains two. Thirty-three scored, W10 D2 L21. Full strength W0 D0 L12; skill 0 W8 D1 L2; 1800 setting W2 D1 L7. Referee status: provisional engine-relative Elo 1570, 95% credible interval 1380–1770; twenty-one calibrated and twelve excluded. No sustained improvement established.
Developed d4/e3/c4/Be2 and castled. Central trades retained material until the bishop/knight exchange sequence: Bc4 Nb4 Bxe6 Nxc2 matched queens; Bf5 g6 Rd2 gxf5 Rxc2 matched my bishop for the knight. That vacated e4's bishop defense, allowing Bxe4 and a pawn deficit. Rd2 saved the rook from the bishop's diagonal and retained Nf3's real recapture. King centralization met discovered rook checks with Kd3/Kc4. Rxd2 Nxd2 exchanged the last rooks. b4 Bxg2 lost another pawn; the king had left g1 and my knight had left f3. bxc5 Bc6 Nb3 bxc5 Kxc5 recovered a pawn, and Nd4 f4 Nf5+ Kf8 Nxh6 recovered another. Both sides then had three pawns.
Fresh ending failures: Bc8 restricted all knight exits. I played h3 to support Ng4, but Bxh3 simply took that unsupported pawn; f3 afterward provided the alternative support. Be6 Kd6 Kg7 Ng4 Bxg4 fxg4 traded the minor pieces. I wrongly expected Ke5/Kf4 to catch the f-pawn. Actual a5 Ke5 f3 Kf4 f2 Kf3 f1=Q+ promoted: approaching f2 on f3 does not capture it, and the pawn had the next move. Long earlier speculative branches did not prevent this elementary tempo error. The active king on d6 was too far away for that route.
Continued against skill 0 despite the queen deficit. Kg3/Kh4 and g5 retained a king-supported pawn. Kh5 interposed g5 between the queen and king, which caused the illegal pawn push above. Black's queen checks ultimately repeated the same positions; Kh4 on ply 111 triggered the referee's threefold draw. This was an opponent-dependent practical salvage, not a demonstrated drawn pawn ending or successful promotion defense. Every move chosen by me; no outside advice, sources, delegation, automated chess analysis or measured token counter. Records retained.

Trial 31: remembered benchmark-33 position at ply 78, before 40.Kd6. Choose Kc5-d4 instead, moving toward the advanced f4 pawn before exchanging the minor pieces. ## Benchmark 34 — Black, skill 0, win by checkmate (80 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-four scored, W11 D2 L21. Full strength W0 D0 L12; skill 0 W9 D1 L2; 1800 setting W2 D1 L7. Referee provisional engine-relative Elo 1580, 95% credible interval 1400–1770; twenty-two calibrated, twelve excluded. Illegal totals remain seven benchmark and two practice. Not evidence of sustained improvement by itself.
English c4/e3/Nc3/Be2: e5/Nf6/Nc6/d5/Be7/Nxd5, castled, Be6/Qd7/Rad8 completed development. f5/f4/fxe3/fxe3 exchanged the f-pawn for White's e-pawn. Bf6 supported e5 and prepared the diagonal to c3. Nxc3 Qxc3 e4 unmasked that bishop against the queen while attacking Nf3. Nd4 interposed; Bxd4 exd4 Qxd4+ won a pawn. exd3 gained a second, but Rxf8+ Rxf8 Bxd3 recovered one for White. Important corrected calculation: after Rd8 recaptured on f8, it no longer supported d3. Qxd3 would allow Qxd3 with no recapture. I instead traded queens Qxc3 bxc3. Material rook/bishop/knight against rook/two bishops, one-pawn advantage.
Rd8 attacked Bd3; Bf4 left it undefended. Rxd3 Bxc7 won a bishop while White recovered a pawn. Bd5/c4/Be4 retained the bishop and its g2 diagonal. Rd2 Bd6 Bxg2+ Kg1 won g2 with the bishop backed by the rook. Bxf1 captured the remaining rook; White Bc5 declined Kxf1. Bxc4 escaped the king attack and captured another pawn. Nxb4 captured the remaining bishop, and h4 declined axb4. Nd3 saved the knight from that pawn capture, with Bc4's support. These were substantial skill-0 errors; I do not claim the preceding attack was forced against better defense.
The final conversion retained all pieces. Bd5+ forced Kg1. Kf7/Kf6/Kf5/Kf4 brought the king closer while Rd2 restricted rank two. Bg2 and Kg3 gave the bishop a king defender independent of the rook. Nf2 h6 Rd1# finished: Nf2 supported Rd1 and covered h1, Bg2 covered f1/h1, Kg3 covered f2/g2/h2. White's a6/h6 pawns did not supply a check or block. Referee confirmed mate. Earlier mental branches repeatedly confused diagonal rook contact with check and failed to see how Nf2 would block Rd2's rank before the king reached g3; I corrected those before submission. The actual king support mattered, not a speculative mating label.
Trial-31 transfer assessment: no analogous close promotion race occurred. I let White advance a/h pawns only after counting that the final mating check arrived before promotion. This narrow successful count does not establish general improvement in passer defense. Legal geometry held this game; whole-position guard counting remains unreliable in long speculative branches. All moves mine, no external sources/advice/delegation or automated chess analysis, no measured token counter.

Trial 32 outcome, practice 25: Nd6 Bg2 Kd4 Kg7 Ne4 Kg6 Ke5 Bxe4 Kxe4 Kh5 Kxf4 Kg6. Ten-ply horizon extended to twelve to resolve the immediate pawn capture. No illegal submission. White king and a2/f2/h2 against Black king and a7/f7/h6, material equal. Nd6 retained a king defender initially and avoided the attacked exits on h6. Kd4 moved toward f4, although Bg2 prevented Ke4 directly. Ne4 was defended by Kd4, then Ke5 retained that knight's defense; Bxe4 Kxe4 matched bishop and knight. Black's Kh5 left f4 undefended, and Kxf4 captured it legally before a push. My own f2 pawn remained a physical blocker throughout, unlike the earlier fxg4 trade which vacated f2. That difference, not a faster king alone, also prevented the original promotion sequence. Bg2 did not capture a pawn: g2 had already been emptied in the benchmark before this saved position.
Target achieved over the observed horizon: matching minor-piece trade, no promotion, no own pawn loss, opponent f-pawn removed. Skill-5 remembered position and opponent Kh5 choice; no proof of equality, best play, won ending or fresh transfer. The remaining king/pawn ending was not continued. Drill stopped unfinished and retained.
Decision for benchmark 35: consider knight mobility and the resulting pawn structure before an edge pawn capture; do not trade away a pawn blockade while assuming the king can catch the new passer. Separate the actual pawn destination from the king's next capture square in each tempo count. The trial locally corrected two benchmark-33 errors, while benchmark 34's win relied on substantial opponent mistakes and does not test that correction. Return to fixed benchmark 35, White 1800 setting; broader evaluation after 36 remains due. No extra drill first.

## Benchmark 35 — White, 1800 setting, loss by checkmate (74 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-five scored, W11 D2 L22. Full strength W0 D0 L12; skill 0 W9 D1 L2; 1800 setting W2 D1 L8. Referee provisional engine-relative Elo 1570, 95% credible interval 1390–1760; twenty-three calibrated, twelve excluded. Seven benchmark and two practice illegal submissions unchanged. PGN read directly from my own data/games/000035.pgn.gz to confirm final 37.Rad7 Rf2#.
Against d4/c6/Nf3/f5, used c4/g3, met Bb4+ with Bd2/Bxd2+/Qxd2, Bg2/castled. cxd5/exd5, Ne5/f4/e3 developed a pawn-supported central knight. Rae1 did not defend Ne5 through the e3 interposer; d4/f4 were its actual defenders. Na4/Nc5/Nxe6/Rxe6 traded a knight for Black's remaining bishop. a3/Rc1/b4/a4, axb4/Qxb4 matched queen-side pawns. Qxb4 removed e3's queen guard; Rfe1 restored an actual rook guard before Qb6/Qxb6/Nxb6 traded queens. Material remained equal through ply 48: two rooks, bishop/knight and six pawns against two rooks, two knights and six pawns.
Main material failure: 25.Nd7 Nxd7. I considered Nd7's attack on Nb6 while overlooking Nb6's reciprocal attack on d7. My knight had no defender there and no recapture existed. Many long branches about counterplay after Rxa4 or Nxa4 failed to include the immediate simplest response. Lost a whole knight. The two-by-one geometry was legal, but the capture/defender calculation was wrong. This is a fresh failure despite the prior notes about cheap attackers and unsupported pieces.
Continued with Ra1/Rb8/a5/h5/a6/bxa6/Rxa6, retaining equal pawn counts while losing my a-pawn and removing Black's b-pawn. I had also misread b7-a6 capture in speculative reasoning; the actual referee reply corrected it. Nf7 reopened Re6's c6 guard, so I avoided Rxc6 at that point. Ra7/Kf2 and Nd6/Rc1/h4 allowed Rxc6: Nd6 now blocked Re6-c6. Nfe4+ came from f6, not d6, so the rook interposer remained. Kf3/Ree8/Rcc7/Kf8/Rxg7 recovered another pawn. My two seventh-rank rooks supported each other; Black remained a knight ahead with two fewer pawns.
Final royal failure: Rb2 then Rad7 ignored Rf2#. Rf2 checked Kf3 directly, and Ne4 defended f2. E2/G2 were on the checking rook's rank; e3/f4/g3 were occupied by my pawns, g2 by my bishop; Ne4 was defended by d5/f5, and f5 guarded g4. The checker was adjacent, so no block was possible. My king on f3 also obstructed Bg2's diagonal toward e4, repeating the king-as-interposer weakness seen earlier. Earlier reasoning explicitly noticed the king had no legal moves but kept evaluating remote rook/knight branches instead of the immediate rook-to-f2 mate. Long speculative calculation was again ineffective; this must not be reported as improved safety merely because the rook coordination recovered two pawns.
Trial-32 transfer: no trapped h6 knight occurred, but I still moved an undefended knight onto a square controlled by the knight it attacked. Avoiding one remembered edge trap did not generalize to reliable knight mobility or contact checks. Queen/pawn guard replacements and one concrete rook interposer check did work locally; they did not prevent the decisive material or royal failures. No sustained improvement established. All moves mine; no external sources/advice/delegation, automated chess analysis or measured token counter; all records retained.

Again I explored Rc3 checks and overlooked the direct rook-to-d2 mating check. Trial 34 outcome, practice 27: Kf1 Rf2+ Kg1 Rb1+ Bf1 Rbxf1#. Six plies, practice loss by mate, no illegal submission; PGN read through the practice interface. Kf1 avoided the original Rd2 enclosure, but Kg1 moved into a new back-rank enclosure. I favored keeping the bishop king-defended and overlooked Rb1+. At that check Bf1 was the only block I found; Rbxf1 was supported by the other rook on f2, so Kxf1 was illegal. H1 was on the checking rook's rank, f2 was defended by Ne4, g2 by Rf2, and h2 occupied by my own pawn. Trial's survival/bishop-retention target failed. My considered Ke1 after Rf2+ was not played or tested; do not label it a saved alternative.
Twenty-seven practice sessions retained: twenty-four stopped unfinished and three scored losses by mate (18, 26, 27). Illegal practice total remains two. This pair of drills added failure evidence: changing one king destination did not make my immediate rook-check calculation reliable, and the supported checking rook was repeatedly missed. No favorable practice result or transfer claim. No infrastructure fault, external assistance or automated chess analysis.
Decision for benchmark 36: revise the ineffective long-branch method. Before submitting an attacking move, inspect the opponent's immediate checks on the actual current board, particularly a rook landing adjacent to the king; name its actual defender and the king's available destination. Do not replace this with remote attack branches. Before placing a knight in contact with another knight, verify the reciprocal capture and my recapture; Nd7/Nxd7 lost a piece without any recapture despite lengthy calculations. This is a behavioral trial to measure in the next fresh game, not evidence of learning itself. Return now to fixed benchmark 36, Black 1800 setting, then assess the full games-31–36 rotation. No further remembered drill before that benchmark.

## Benchmark 36 — Black, 1800 setting, loss by resignation (139 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-six scored, W11 D2 L23. Full strength W0 D0 L12; skill 0 W9 D1 L2; 1800 setting W2 D1 L9. Authoritative provisional engine-relative Elo 1560, 95% credible interval 1380–1740; twenty-four calibrated and twelve full-strength excluded. Seven illegal benchmark submissions and two practice submissions unchanged. Own PGN read to confirm the final sequence. Resigned after 70.Ra8 with king/four pawns against king/rook/four pawns, a full rook deficit. This was a practical resignation, not a proof of forced mate or an engine evaluation.
London setup: d5/Nf6/e6/Bd6/castled, c5/Nc6/b6/Bb7/Rc8 developed. Bb7 replaced b7's pawn defense of Nc6; Qe7 then Qd7 retained Bd6's actual queen defender while removing Bh4's queen pin on Nf6. Ne4 was defended by d5, but Bxe4 dxe4 Nxe4 traded my knight for a bishop and lost a pawn. This pawn loss was anticipated after the move, not successfully prevented. Na5 vacated c6 and opened Bb7's attack on Ne4 while retaining b6's pawn defender. c4 supplied space; Nxd6 Qxd6 matched knight/bishop. f5/Bd5/Qc6/Nb7/Nd6, Be5/Rfd8/Bxd6/Rxd6 matched another minor pair. Recapturing with the rook retained Qc6 temporarily; later Qc7 freed the bishop's retreat before e4/fxe4/fxe4/Bb7. I rejected Bxe4 after noticing the e-file queen/rook recaptures. b5/a4/a6, axb5/axb5 retained a cheap c4 pawn defender.
Rf8/Rf6/Rxf6/gxf6 traded one rook pair. Ng4 attacked f6 and h6. Qg7 defended both; Kf7/Kg6/Kh7 supplied actual king defenders while avoiding the queen's g-file discoveries. Rd7 escaped Qg3's attack on the rook. e5/fxe5/Qxe5/Qxe5/Rxe5 traded queens, leaving rook/bishop/four pawns against rook/knight/five pawns. Rd6 defended e6; Rc5/Bc6/Ne5/Be8 moved the bishop away from a second attacker while preserving b5 defense. Bf7/Bg6 and king moves maintained real recaptures against the central knight, rather than treating a defended rook as safe against a cheaper knight. h5 was bishop-supported; White's g5+ later required Kf5. The g-pawn advanced to g6, supported by both rook and knight. I correctly avoided Bxg6 when the rook would prevent the king recapture, and kept Be8 defended with a back-rank rook.
The ending remained difficult and one pawn down. White's central knight, rook on g8 and passed pawn constrained my bishop and king. Rc8/Ke7/Kf6/Ke7/Kd7 changed defender routes repeatedly. My analysis continued to sprawl over many conditional branches despite the stated shorter-reply trial, and often lost track of actual piece guards before correction. No claim of a proved fortress, saved ending, or best moves. There were no legality rejections, but that does not establish tactical correctness.
Decisive failure: 66...Kd7 67.Kf4 Bxf7 68.gxf7 Ke7 69.Rxc8. Kd7 had supplied Rc8's sole king defender. After the bishop left e8 and was captured, the eighth rank was completely open from White's rook on g8 to Rc8. Ke7 abandoned the rook's defender. I concentrated on f8 promotion and a hypothetical rook/king recapture sequence, missing the immediate Rxc8. The rook was taken for free. Kxf7 legally removed the passed pawn afterward, but did not recover the rook. This is the same source-departure/sole-defender weakness targeted in earlier trials, now involving a king rather than a pawn or interposing piece. My untested idea of moving the rook to d8 at that late position also needs a complete promotion-tempo check; do not label it a saved alternative.
Fresh-method assessment: some local defender replacements, queen retreats and check responses worked without piece loss for a long stretch. The last king move still lost a whole rook, and the shorter immediate-reply method was not reliably followed in my reasoning. No fresh mating loss this game, but that narrow fact does not constitute improved king safety or transfer. No external sources/advice/delegation or automated chess search/scoring/analysis/move selection; all moves mine, all records retained, no measured token counter submitted.

## Evaluation after 36
Games 31–36: L, L, D, W, L, L = W1 D1 L4, 1.5/6. Calibrated games 33–36 scored 1.5/4; both full-strength games lost. Previous 25–30 block was W3 D1 L2, 3.5/6, with calibrated 3.5/4. Elo 1590 after 30 to 1560 after 36, with strongly overlapping credible intervals (1390–1780 versus 1380–1740). This block does not establish a statistically resolved decline, but it provides no evidence of sustained improvement. All twelve full-strength benchmarks remain losses. The two latest 1800 games failed through an unsupported knight and an abandoned rook defender, with a mating net also missed in game 35.
Remembered drills in this period achieved some short local targets (king off the c-file, earlier passer blockade, avoiding the h6 knight trap). Others lost by mate (26 and 27). Repetition recovery against skill 0 and a skill-0 win depended on substantial opponent errors. Those favorable outcomes do not validate the method. Long analysis and repeated checklists have not made the recurring geometry/recapture/source-vacancy errors reliable.
Next evaluation after 42, the next complete fixed rotation. Change the learning emphasis to a concrete departure example with observable retention and recapture, rather than another general checklist. Trial outcomes remain hypotheses tested against recorded practice replies; fresh games 37–42 will measure transfer. No claim that a different inference budget is being measured.

Trial 35: remembered benchmark-36 position at ply 131, after 66.Nf7 and before ...Kd7. Choose Rc8-c7 instead, keeping the king on e7 (where it can directly recapture on f7 after a matching minor-piece trade) and moving the rook off White's open eighth-rank capture route. Ten plies against skill 5, Black. Target: retain the rook or complete a matching rook trade, and record how the passed pawn is handled; no unrecaptured Rxc8 loss. Inspect the exact defender lost by every king/rook departure and the actual enemy capture before considering promotion branches. This earlier alternative is unverified and may still fail; not a best-move or equality claim. Return afterward to benchmark 37, White full strength, with the next broader evaluation after 42.

Trial 35 outcome, practice 28: Rc7 Rh8 Bxf7 gxf7 Kxf7 Rh7+ Kg6 Rxc7. Eight plies, no illegal submission. The passed pawn was removed and the minor pieces traded, but the rook-retention target failed. Rh7+ put my king between the checking rook and my own undefended rook on c7. Any ordinary king departure opened Rh7-c7; Kg6 did so, and Rxc7 took the rook without a recapture. I repeated the king-as-interposer/skewer error while believing that moving off the eighth rank had fixed the measurement failure. The stated ten-ply horizon was stopped at eight because the decisive target failure had already occurred. This is an unfinished stopped drill with an observed rook loss, not a scored practice loss or a successful defense. Twenty-eight practice sessions retained: twenty-five stopped unfinished and three scored losses by mate. Illegal totals unchanged.

Trial 36: repeat benchmark 36 at the same ply 131, skill 5, Black. Choose Rc8-c6 instead of Rc7, keeping the rook away from the seventh rank that the king would occupy after Kxf7. The king remains on e7 to defend Be8 and recapture on f7 if the same trade occurs. Ten-ply target: retain the rook or match a rook trade, remove or safely blockade the passer, and inspect the checking rook's line beyond the king after every king departure. Trial 36 outcome, practice 29: Rc6 Ne5 Rb6 Kf4 b4 g7 Bf7 Ra8 bxc3 Ra7+ Kf6 Rxf7#. Ten-ply horizon extended to twelve to answer the final check, then practice loss by mate. No illegal submission. The extension exposed the same adjacent supported rook check as the earlier failed drills. Do not grade the trial successful on the narrower rook-retention fact.
Rf7 captured my bishop and checked Kf6 directly. Ne5 defended f7, e6 was my own pawn, Ne5 was supported by d4 and Kf4, the checking rook controlled e7/g7/f5, and h4 controlled g5. G6 was controlled by Ne5. No king capture of the checker, block, or other check response existed. I had calculated knight/bishop recapture cases but missed the immediate rook capture with knight support after Ra7+. Kd6 and Ke8 were candidate legal alternatives in my post-game reasoning, not played, independently verified, or proved saved. This is a fresh failed extension of a remembered drill; no best-move claim or infrastructure fault.
Twenty-nine recorded practice sessions now: twenty-five stopped unfinished and four scored losses by mate (18, 26, 27, 29). Seven illegal benchmark submissions and two practice submissions unchanged. All started records retained; practice does not alter benchmark results or Elo.
Decision for benchmark 37: the revised trials did not make source-vacancy checks or immediate supported rook checks reliable. For the next fresh game, make the actual opponent's direct check/capture the first calculation, including the checker destination's support and the king's legal escape. If a king departs, check the target beyond its old square before evaluating a promotion or attack. Avoid remote branches that substitute conditional coordinates for the actual board. This is a change to test, not evidence it works. No further remembered drill before fixed benchmark 37, White full strength. Next broader evaluation after 42 remains fixed.

## Benchmark 37 — White, full strength, resignation (54 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-seven scored: W11 D2 L24; full strength W0 D0 L13, skill 0 W9 D1 L2, 1800 setting W2 D1 L9. Provisional engine-relative Elo unchanged at 1560, 95% credible interval 1380–1740; twenty-four calibrated games, thirteen excluded. 163 scored games remain. All records retained.
The d4/c4 opening reached an isolated d-pawn. Nxb3 axb3 exchanged bishop for knight; Nxd5 Nxd5 Bxh4 used an intermediate bishop capture. g3/Bb7/gxh4/Bxd5 traded the next minor pair and left a pawn deficit. Re1 Bxf3 Qxf3 removed my knight, which had been h4's actual defender. Qf3 does not defend h4 (a knight-shaped displacement); Qxh4 won another pawn. Qg3/Qf6/Ra2 defended b2 from the queen diagonal. Qe5/Rd1 pinned Re1 to Kg1, invalidating its nominal defense of Qe5 until Kg2 unpinned it. Qg6+ Kh3 then exposed the king to rook checks with few flights.
Rd5 attacked Qe5; Qg3 was supported by Kh3 and the f2/h2 pawns. This did not make the queen safe against a cheaper rook. Rg5 attacked Qg3, backed by Qg6. I played f4, thinking the pawn attack forced the rook to retreat. Rxg3+ instead traded Black's rook for my queen. Kxg3 was illegal because Qg6 defended the checking rook. hxg3 recaptured, but Black had no obligation to play Qxg3+; Rd8 kept its queen. I resigned with two rooks/four pawns against queen/rook/six pawns, no concrete counterplay identified. This is a practical resignation, not a forced-mate proof.
The long conditional calculation missed an immediate profitable capture and incorrectly treated an optional recapture as forced. Legality improved in this game, tactical correctness did not. No evidence of transfer or sustained improvement. Every chess decision my own; no outside assistance, automated analysis or measured token counter.

Kf1 re-pins Re1 to the king, so its nominal queen defense must not be counted without checking legality. This remembered alternative is my unverified choice, not a best-move or equality claim. Return afterward to benchmark 38, Black full strength; broader evaluation after 42.

My king on e2 also blocked Qe3's e-file route. No success claim, no saved-position claim, and no further repetition before fresh evaluation. Decision for benchmark 38: these attempts continued missing immediate mate even against skill 5. Before selecting a quiet move, identify the opponent's direct checking destinations and the king's actual responses from the resulting position. Piece defense alone is not safety: cheaper attackers may trade profitably, and friendly pieces can occupy the only flight squares. Limit remote branches; reconstruct from the actual referee board at each reply. This is a revised method to test, not evidence of learning. Return now to Black full strength. Next broader evaluation after 42.

## Benchmark 38 — Black, full strength, resignation (45 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-eight scored: W11 D2 L25. Full strength W0 D0 L14; skill 0 W9 D1 L2; 1800 W2 D1 L9. Elo 1560, 95% credible interval 1380–1740; twenty-four calibrated games and fourteen excluded. 162 remain.
Closed Ruy Lopez: a6/Nf6/Be7/b5/d6/castle/Bb7/Re8/Na5/c5/Qc7. White b3 restrained Na5. My c4 occupied its escape square, while Bb7 occupied the other retreat. b4 then attacked the knight; Nc6 dxc6 Bxc6 lost it for a pawn. This was a fresh own-piece obstruction/knight trap, not an unavoidable opening loss. d5 exd5 Bxd5 Nxe5 recovered White's remaining pawn deficit. I correctly declined Qxe5 because exd5 had cleared White's e-file and Re1 now defended Ne5. Bd6/Ng4/Nxg4/hxg4 matched the remaining black knight for a white knight.
Rad8 Rxe8+ Rxe8 Qxd5 then lost my bishop. Its former defender Nf6 had been exchanged; the rook on d8 was blocked from d5 by my own Bd6. The imagined Bh2+ tempo after Qxd5 depended on Rd8 remaining on its file; White exchanged rooks first. Rxe8 left no recapture of Qxd5. I resigned queen/rook/bishop/six pawns against queen/rook/two bishops/knight/six pawns: two minor pieces down, no concrete compensation identified. Practical resignation, not a forced-mate proof. The immediate-check method did not prevent the knight trap or the undefended bishop loss. No transfer/improvement claim.

## Benchmark 39 — White, skill 0, win by checkmate (69 plies)
No illegal submissions. Thirty-nine scored: W12 D2 L25. Full strength W0 D0 L14; skill 0 W10 D1 L2; 1800 W2 D1 L9. Philidor setup: d4/Nf3/Bc4/Nc3, exd4/Nxd4; Ne5/Bb3/c5/Nf3 answered the pawn attack before castling. Bg4 pinned Nf3 to Qd1; Qe1 removed that relative pin. Nxe5 Be6 declined the pawn recapture, allowing Nf3 to save the knight. c4/Ba4/d5/exd5/Qxd5 placed Black's queen under Nc3: Nxd5 won it, Nh5 declined another recapture, and Nxe7+ won the bishop. Bg5 supported Ne7; Qb4 replaced its support after f6 forced Be3. Qb5/Bg4/Qxh5/Bxh5 then lost my queen for a knight. I saw the bishop on g4 but failed to count its immediate diagonal defense of h5. This repeats the direct capture/value failure from game 37; the win does not demonstrate a successful safety method.
Still three minor pieces ahead, Nd4 supported a retreat to Nef5 when Rbe8 attacked Ne7. Re4/Ng3 forked the rook and bishop. Rh4/Nxh5/h6 declined the knight recapture. Nf4/g5/Ng6+ forked king and both rooks. Kg7/Nxf8/Rxd4/Bxd4/Kxf8 exchanged my two knights for both enemy rooks. Black's bishop and rooks were gone; White retained two rooks and two bishops against king/six pawns. These were substantial opponent errors, not a proved forced winning line against stronger defense.
Rad1/Rfe1 coordinated the rooks. Bc3 vacated the d-file, remained supported by b2, and physically blocked c4-c3. Rd8+ Kf7 Rd7+ Kg8 Re8# completed the conversion. Rd7 cut rank seven; Re8 checked along rank eight, leaving no king flight. All moves chosen by me, no external study/advice/delegation or automated analysis, all records retained, no measured token counter.

Developed d5/Nf6/e6/c5/Nc6/Be7/castle/b6/Bb7/Rc8. Ne4/Bxe4/dxe4 traded my knight for White's bishop; this vacated d5 and removed c4's pawn defense. Nd8 opened Bb7's diagonal to defend e4 without prematurely moving f7, which still defended e6 against Nf4. c4/Bd6/c5/Bxf4/cxb6/Qxb6/exf4 completed matching minor and pawn trades. Nc6/Re1/f5 now defended e4 cheaply after the f4 knight was gone. Bb2/Qd3/Re3/Qd5 kept the queen out of attacks by cheaper pieces. g6 blocked Rg3's path and was defended by h7. Nd4/Re1/Nc2 forked Qe3 and Re1; Qe2/Nxe1 won the rook, and Qh5 declined the knight recapture. I did not submit gxh5: g6 was absolutely pinned to Kg8 by Rg3. Nd3 attacked undefended Bb2. Rxg6+ hxg6 Qxg6+ destroyed my shelter but gave up White's last rook. fxe5/Nxb2 captured the bishop; Nxe4/fxe4 captured the remaining knight. These were major opponent errors, not a forced conversion against better play.
The large material advantage still risked repetition. With Rf8 occupying f8, Qg6+/Qh6+ forced g8/h8 king replies. Qg7+ Rg6 Qh6 Rxh6 finally captured the undefended queen. a4/Rc1# mated: the c-rook checked along rank one, and Kg3 controlled f2/g2/h2. All accepted PGN read. No external advice/sources/delegation or automated analysis; all records retained, no measured token counter.

## Benchmark 42 — Black, 1800 setting, checkmate loss (109 plies)
PGN verified; zero illegal submissions. Forty-two scored: W13 D2 L27. Full strength W0 D0 L14; skill 0 W11 D1 L2; 1800 setting W2 D1 L11. Provisional engine-relative Elo 1560, 95% credible interval 1390–1720; 28 calibrated games, 14 excluded. 158 scored games remain. All records preserved.
The opening reached a temporary extra pawn after f6/fxe5. Qf5 allowed Ncd6, forking Qf5 and Re8. Correction to my commentary and private assessment: Nd6 did NOT attack Rf8; d6-f8 is a two-by-two displacement, not a knight move. I initially called this a fork of both rooks and anticipated an unavoidable exchange loss incorrectly. Qh7/Re7 preserved both rooks; White took b7 instead. Be8/Bxe8/Rfxe8 matched bishops, freeing the queen's h-file route.
Nb6 vacated d5 but kept the knight backed by a7. Rf3/Rxd3/Rxa3 won pawns. b5 attacked Nc6, whose retreat would remove Re7's knight guardian while Qg5 attacked that rook. Rc7/bxc6 lost the knight; I failed to anticipate the pawn advance and its retreat constraints. Qxe5/Qxd4 removed the compensating central pawns. This is my unverified account of the tactical problem, not an engine assessment or proof that the loss was unavoidable earlier.
Ne7 later supported Rxc6, removing White's passed pawn without an unmatched rook loss. Rac4 connected the rooks; a6 retained that pawn under Rc6's actual horizontal defense. Qf5/Ng6/Qxf5/exf5 matched queens. I reached two rooks and one knight/four pawns versus two rooks and two knights/three pawns. King activation failed: Kf8 had defended Ne7, but Kg7 departed that guardian. Rd7 pinned Ne7 and added a second rook attacker alongside Re5. Kf6 did not save Ne7: Rdxe7 was supported by Re5, so the king could not recapture. The rook pair and my own f5/g6/h5 pawns then boxed Kf6. Rc2 pinned Nf2 but did not prevent Ng5-h7#.
Mate geometry: Nh7 checked Kf6 as a knight. White's Re7 controlled e7/f7/g7 and supported Re5; Re5 controlled e6, and f4 supported e5. f4 and h4 both controlled g5. My own pawns occupied f5/g6, and no rook could capture Nh7. Knight checks cannot be interposed against. The loss was already anticipated before Rc2, not an infrastructure fault. My long calculations repeatedly imported conditional or previous-game coordinates; I caught some phantom pawn and knight-geometry errors but did not make the checks reliable. Every submitted move was legal, which is not evidence of adequate tactical play.

## Benchmark 44 — Black, full strength, mate loss (45 plies)
PGN/status verified, no illegal submissions. Forty-four scored: W13 D2 L29. Full strength W0 D0 L16; skill 0 W11 D1 L2; 1800 W2 D1 L11. Provisional engine-relative Elo unchanged 1560, 95% credible interval 1390–1720; 28 calibrated, 16 excluded. 156 remain. All losses preserved; no infrastructure fault.
I chose a Caro-Kann Advance structure. h5 blocked White's h-pawn; e6/Bxf5/exf5 matched the dark bishops. Nh6/Be7/Nd7 developed around White's e5 restriction on d6/f6. Ng4 restored the h-file rook line, and g6 gave f5/h5 cheap pawn defenses. Both sides castled. Ndxe5/dxe5/Nxe5 gave two central pawns for one knight and attacked Qd3 on both knight captures; this was a deliberate compensation trial, not a proved sound sacrifice. Rb8 guarded b7. Nxd3/Qxd3 matched the next knight pair; Bxg5/Bxg5 matched my bishop for White's remaining knight and exposed Qd8. Qb6 escaped under a7's actual pawn defense.
Qxb2/Qxc2/Qxa2 took three undefended queenside pawns after Rae1 removed a2's rook defender. At that point I had queen/two rooks/eight pawns versus queen/two rooks/bishop/three pawns. The material count was not a position evaluation, and the extra pawns did not protect the king. Qe5/Bh6 created Qg7#; I answered Bh6's attack on Rf8 with Rfe8 and missed the immediate queen mate.
Critical factual failure: I repeatedly assessed Qg7 as unsupported using the bishop's FORMER g5 square. On the ACTUAL board the bishop was h6, directly defending g7. The g6 pawn did not defend g7; it was not an interposer on the e5-f6-g7 route. Qg7 controlled f8/h8/f7/h7, f7 was also my own pawn, and Bh6 made Kxg7 illegal. No queen or rook could capture the adjacent checker, and an adjacent queen check cannot be blocked. The referee recorded Qg7#.
This is fresh failure of the same actual-board versus conditional-coordinate problem, despite prolonged calculation and zero illegal moves. I spent substantial reasoning on distant pawn/rook branches while overlooking mate in one on the actual board. The short successful practice 37 did not transfer to king safety in this stronger game. No evidence of sustained improvement, no best-move/equality claim, and no invented resource counter. All chess decisions mine; no external advice/study/delegation or automated analysis/search/scoring/move selection.

Advance Caro-Kann: Bxd3/Qxd3 matched light bishops. Bxh6/Rxh6 matched knight/dark bishop while keeping the g-pawn; Qc7/queenside castling were legal. Bxg5/hxg5 matched the last bishop/white knight, leaving a knight each. But hxg5 attacked Rh6. Rg6 avoided the pawn capture while becoming caged by own e6/g7 and enemy g5's f6/h6 fields; after f4 the g5 pawn also had pawn support. c5/c4 chased Qd3 but did not solve rook mobility. Nf8 supplied a g6 guard before ...f6 departed its pawn guard; Rh3 Rxh5 won the undefended h-pawn. Qf7 restored the rook guard, Nd7 and ...fxg5 fxg5 relocated White's f-pawn and opened its rook's f-file onto Qf7. Qe8 evaded that lower-valued rook while retaining a diagonal guard of g6. Nb6/Nf4 then attacked the caged rook. I proposed Qh8 as pressure on Rh5, overlooking the reciprocal lower-valued rook capture Rxh8: Rd8's guard merely allowed Rxh8 recapture, a queen-for-rook loss. After Rxh8 the actual reply was Qxg6, not the imagined Nxg6. Rook lost unmatched after its queen guard departed. At resignation R+N+6P versus Q+R+N+7P: an extra queen and pawn for White. Practical assessment, no forced-mate assertion.
The serious source/guard target failed again. Many long unplayed branches considered possible future checks while missing a direct rook capture of the moved queen. A guarded queen is still unsafe against a cheaper rook when the recapture only trades queen for rook. Legality alone and several cautious opening choices did not prevent the queen blunder. All results preserved.

6...Bb4 placed the bishop where White's queen a4 could attack it. After the matched Nxd5 Qxd5, Bxc6 attacked my queen while removing Bb4's knight guardian. Rab8 defended b7 while abandoning a7; ...b5 Qxa7 lost that pawn. Later rook and queen exchanges matched, and ...g5 hxg5 lost another pawn after queens were gone. Final actual material: own R+B+four pawns b4,c7,e5,h7 versus White R+B+N+six pawns a2,b2,e3,f2,g2,g5. Thus knight and TWO pawns down at resignation; my public statement of knight and one pawn down referred incorrectly to the earlier count before hxg5. Practical resignation, not forced-mate evidence. Zero illegal submissions does not mean tactically sound play. No external assistance or infrastructure fault.

Repeated rook attacks required bishop retreats. The queenside pawns drove Ra4–a3–a1 and a rook exchange. Kf1 placed the king behind Rd3 on the c4–d3–e2–f1 diagonal; after bxa3 Bc4 the rook was pinned and attacked by the cheaper bishop. Ke2/Ke3 added a legal king recapture and removed the pin, but I did not exploit that moment effectively. Rdd1 Rxa3+ Rxa3 Rxa3+ led to an additional rook trade and a continuing check. Kd2 Bb5 Kc2 Ba4+ Kd2 Bxd1 Kxd1 lost the last rook for a bishop; Rd3+ Ke2 Rxd4 then removed d4. At resignation: two restricted bishops and three pawns versus rook, knight, and five pawns. Practical loss assessment, not a forced-mate proof.
I again spent substantial reasoning on unplayed branches while missing direct threats or constraining my own rooks with the king. The shorter immediate-capture approach remains inconsistently implemented. No sustained transfer from the short remembered drills is demonstrated.
